#2969. Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season

Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Before the bye week, Saquon Barkley was battling through hamstring and back injuries for a few games.

As the Eagles get ready for the second half of their season, Barkley is definitely feeling healthier. But it doesn't sound like he's 100 percent, nor will he be.

"I wish the bye week was like a magic pill and everything just vanishes," Barkley said. "But that's only in la-la land, I guess. But it definitely did help."

Barkley, 29, has had a disappointing season. Injuries have limited him, but he has just 69 rushing attempts for 296 yards and 3 touchdowns. It's obviously not all his fault, of course. Barkley is playing behind a struggling offensive line in a dysfunctional offense.

But he also doesn't think he is personally playing to his capabilities.

"I think the biggest thing you could do in a bye week is go back and watch yourself on film," Barkley said. "Watch other football, watch other games and see how well other backs are doing. And just be honest with yourself. If I'm being honest with myself, the last couple weeks, for whatever reason, I don't like the way I was playing and what I was putting out on the field.

"The way I go about changing that is the only way I know how to, just put my head down and go to work, just continue to be consistent. But it's got to look different. It's got to look different than what it was in '24 and '25 because every season is going to be different challenges. Felt really good this week to be able to have a full week, go out there and practice and put the work in. And hopefully I see the reward on Sunday."

The last two games for Barkley haven't gone well. Against the Commanders, he had 13 carries for 30 yards and against the Giants, he had 13 carries for 22 yards. His yards-per-carry average dropped from 5.6 to 4.3 on the season after those two losses.

So Barkley and his teammates happily welcomed a bye week on the heels of a seven-game losing streak.

"I think it was helpful for everyone," Barkley said. "From staff to everyone that works in this building to players. Sometimes when a season is not going the way you wanted it to go and you're facing a lot of adversity, that week off is really important. More so for just the mind also. Obviously, the body. It's good for the body. But just the mind and getting your mind somewhere else a little bit. And then regroup and come back ready to work."

As the Eagles look toward the last eight games of the season, they are in the basement of the NFC East with a 2-7 record. They are not technically dead just yet, but it would take a mini miracle for them to pull themselves out of this.

But that's easy to say on the outside. For the guys in the locker room, they have to keep fighting even after they eventually get eliminated. That starts with Sunday against a Steelers team that ranks third in the NFL in rushing defense.

"One game at a time," Barkley said. "I said it already, I'm going to say it again. We put ourselves in this situation. Only way we're going to get out of it is by digging ourselves out. I still believe we have the men and women in this locker room and in this building to get it done. … Technically, we're still in the division and obviously has that crossed my mind? Of course. But can't even take it there.

"Just have to be focused on one game at at time and staying present and taking advantage of the opportunities and this opportunity we have coming up against a really good team who's trying to get back on track and definitely didn't like the way that they played on national television."

#2968. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2966. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2961. Colts release starting QB Daniel Jones less than a year after extending him

Colts release starting QB Daniel Jones less than a year after extending him originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Daniel Jones played so well in his first 10 games with the Indianapolis Colts that he entered the MVP conversation.

His last 11 games were so bad, he lost his job.

On Friday, less than 24 hours after yet another abysmal offensive performance in a 26-3 loss at Houston, Colts coach Shane Steichen said the team was releasing Jones and turning to Anthony Richardson as the starter when it faces the New York Giants next week. It's the second time in three years Jones has been released in November, the first coming after he sought and received his release from the Giants in 2024.

Jones then joined the Minnesota Vikings practice squad before signing a one-year deal with the Colts in March 2025 and a two-year deal worth $88 million earlier this year. This time, though, Indy — not Jones — made the call.

"This was a decision that was made for, you know, the best interests of both parties," Steichen said, explaining Jones had not asked for his release. "It's just where we're at in our season. That's just the situation we're at right now."

For the Colts (3-8), it's another key move in what already has been a tumultuous season.

Team co-owner Carlie Irsay-Gordon fired general manager Chris Ballard last month, and Indy then traded four key players — All-Pro guard Quenton Nelson, Pro Bowl defensive tackle DeForest Buckner, Pro Bowl receiver Keenan Allen and receiver Josh Downs — before this month's trading deadline. Nelson and Buckner were captains and longtime locker room mainstays while Allen and Downs were Indy's top two receivers.

Now they're cutting ties with Jones, who was going to be a $46.8 million salary cap hit next season. Instead, the Colts will save about $11.6 million, as they give Richardson, the No. 4 overall pick in the 2023 draft, a second chance to prove himself. Steichen said he had spoken with Irsay-Gordon before announcing the move but that he made the final decision.

"I think there's a lot of factors, I don't think it was just on one person," Steichen said. "Obviously, the quarterback takes a lot of the blame, obviously, that's the business we're in. But things, you know, just didn't work out."

Jones started his Colts career fast, leading them to an 8-2 mark on one of the most productive offenses in league history through 10 games. But Jones wasn't the same after breaking a bone in his left leg last November and struggled mightily this season after returning from last December's season-ending torn right Achilles tendon.

His completion percentage, 58.4%, and passer rating, 65.4, rank among the lowest of all NFL starters. He also struggled with turnovers, throwing 13 interceptions and losing three fumbles. The increasingly poor performances had fans calling for a change.

Jones followed his season-worst passer rating of 41.5 in Sunday's 24-22 victory over the one-win Miami Dolphins with his second worst single-game rating this year, 45.9, on Thursday night. He was 12 of 27 for 144 yards with one interception and was sacked for a safety.

Still, Steichen fully supported Jones, insisting he gave Indy the best chance to win.

"I can't say enough good things about Daniel, just the ultimate competitor, ultimate teammate," Steichen said. "The toughness he brought to our football team — I mean the guy played on a broken leg for us last year for three or four games, snapped his Achilles, came back 8 1/2 months later and gave this organization everything he had. I couldn't be more thankful for the time I had with him."

Richardson replaced Jones in the fourth quarter Thursday. He was 0 for 2 with one carry for 2 yards.

Now, the guy who has twice requested to be traded since losing the quarterback battle to Jones during training camp in 2025, will take over. The former Florida star has struggled with accuracy, posting a career completion percentage of just 50.6% while throwing 11 TD passes and 13 interceptions.

Richardson also has battled injuries, playing in just 15 of the Colts' 34 games when he was the starter. This year, he's 2 of 4 for 8 yards in three games.

But now the guy who was once billed as the future face of the franchise will get a new opportunity. Second-year quarterback Riley Leonard is the only other quarterback on Indy's active roster.

"I talked to Anthony today, I told him, obviously, he'll be the starter versus the Giants," Steichen said. "There's, obviously, a world in which Riley could play as well so we'll evaluate that each and every week throughout the season."

#2960. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2958. Pistons vs Raptors Discussion: Game Time, TV, Odds, and More

The Toronto Raptors are scuffling in the first leg of the NBA season, but there is certainly a lot of talent there. Yes, Kawhi Leonard is absent with news of managing knee pain and swelling that surely puts a shiver up the spine of every Toronto fan. But they also still have Scottie Barnes, and he is delivering the young star goods as advertised. He is averaging 23.8 points, 7.4 rebounds, and 4.7 assists a night. On certain nights, he looks like the young superstar that represents his clearly attainable ceiling. On other nights, he and his team's offense struggle and can't seem to get unstuck from the mud. Toronto has dropped six straight, and will be without the services of Barrett tonight.

Watching an Ausar Thompson and Scottie Barnes matchup could be electric. Cade Cunningham is also expected back tonight after sitting out Detroit's win against the New Orleans Pelicans. Duncan Robinson and Ron Holland are out, and doubtful, respectively.

#2954. Future Eagles Fits: Five interior defensive linemen to watch in 2027 NFL draft

Future Eagles Fits: Five interior defensive linemen to watch in 2027 NFL draft originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Welcome back to Future Eagles Fits, our weekly deep dive into the prospects who could eventually find their way to Philadelphia.

Every week, we're going inside the tape and looking at prospects through an Eagles-centric lens — breaking down the athletic profile, positional versatility and scheme fit that could ultimately determine whether a player makes sense in Philly. Along the way, we'll examine how prospects are being evaluated across the league as draft boards begin to take shape and conversations are had with league representatives across the scouting landscape.

For the Eagles, the interior of their defensive line has quickly become a position to monitor as the organization begins to look ahead. A first and second round pick in return for Jordan Davis is outstanding additional draft capital to work with, but it also leaves a significant piece of the defensive front to replace moving forward. Jalen Carter remains the centerpiece of the group, while Moro Ojomo, Ty Robinson, and Byron Young will have opportunities to take on larger roles within the rotation. Still, adding another young, explosive presence to the interior could go a long way toward maintaining the physicality and disruptive ability that has become such an important part of the Eagles' defensive identity.

The good news? It's an intriguing class up front with several players who offer unique skill sets that could make sense for what the Eagles are looking to add. Whether it's finding another high-end interior pass rusher, adding a versatile defensive lineman who can align up and down the front, or bringing in a bigger-bodied presence who can control the line of scrimmage, there are legitimate options to consider.

While there is still a ton of football left to be played and the entirety of the pre-draft process still to play out — which will alter boards — here are five interior defensive linemen to know in the upcoming class — two potential Day 1 targets, two Day 2 prospects and one Day 3 option who could provide some additional juice up front.

Day 1: Xavier Gilliam, Tennessee | 6'2″, 307 lbs (verified measurements)

If you're looking for an interior presence who can immediately change the way an opposing offense has to approach its protection schemes, it starts with Gilliam in this class.

A former transfer from Penn State, Gilliam has developed into one of the most explosive defensive players in the entire country this season and he's currently a top-10 player on my personal board. Below is a quick peek at the type of pop he provides up front.

At nearly 310 pounds, Gilliam has an outstanding combination of lower-body explosiveness, strength and initial quickness off the snap. He doesn't need much space to generate momentum, and his ability to explode out of his stance allows him to create immediate problems for guards attempting to establish angles.

There's a level of violence to his game that really stands out, as well. He plays with outstanding energy, consistently attacks the line of scrimmage and has the type of first-step quickness that can make even well-positioned offensive linemen uncomfortable. He's not simply a defensive tackle who occupies space and looks to disrupt the run game, he has legitimate pass-rush juice and the athletic profile to create negative plays without needing a blitz or additional help from the second level.

For Philly, he and Carter would be a true nightmare to counter due to their explosiveness up front. Add in Ojomo – we'll see if they do in fact extend him – and all three would give the Eagles an outstanding foundation along the line of scrimmage. If you're looking to invest first-round capital in an interior defensive lineman, finding someone who can make an impact in both phases of the game is a pretty good place to start. He's one of the most exciting defensive prospects in the entire class.

Day 1: Devan Thompkins, Alabama | 6'5″, 290 lbs (verified measurements)

Gilliam is the explosive interior disruptor, but Thompkins might be the most versatile defensive lineman in the entire draft.

A transfer from USC to Alabama, Thompkins has the opportunity to be the first interior/edge hybrid selected next April. At 290 pounds, he brings a unique physical profile to the position, and I'm not sure there's another player in this class who offers quite the same flexibility when it comes to moving around a defensive front.

The biggest selling point with Thompkins is that he doesn't necessarily need to be confined to one particular alignment. He can line up directly across from a guard and attack the interior, work outside an offensive tackle, or even operate as a wide-nine defender outside the tight end. It's an incredibly valuable skill set for any defensive coordinator who wants to manufacture different looks, create favorable matchups and keep opposing offensive lines guessing.

There's real power behind his game, too, which matters for young players making the transition to the pro game. It can't all be finesse, and Thompkins has herculean strength to generate movement at the point of attack, and his ability to attack from different alignments makes him particularly difficult to counter from down to down. He has the athleticism to challenge offensive tackles on the outside, but also possesses the strength and power to create problems when working inside against guards.

What makes his evaluation particularly interesting is the number of different ways an NFL coaching staff could utilize him. Want to kick him inside and create a mismatch against a guard? Sure. Line him up outside the tackle and let him use his length and athleticism to get after the quarterback? That's another option. He's the type of player who gives a defensive coordinator the freedom to be creative with personnel and alignment without sacrificing physicality, and for the Eagles, that versatility could be especially appealing. Philadelphia has traditionally valued defensive linemen who can move around the front and create problems from multiple alignments. With Carter and Ojomo already providing interior options, Thompkins could give the Eagles another player who allows them to change their defensive presentation depending on the opponent.

Day 2: A.J. Holmes Jr, Texas Tech | 6'2″, 300 lbs, 32 ⅜ arm (verified measurements)

Holmes is a name whose draft stock has continued to trend upward throughout the process, and there's a lot to like about what he brings to the interior defensive line.

At 300 pounds, he has a compact, powerful frame that allows him to hold his ground against offensive linemen while also providing pass-rush upside.

He's capable of winning against individual blocks with his power using his upper-body strength to create separation and disrupt the timing of opposing run concepts, but he's not a player who needs to rely exclusively on athleticism to make an impact. There's a physical element to his game that allows him to compete at the point of attack and create problems for offensive linemen who aren't prepared for his initial punch.

He's also shown the ability to provide something as a pass rusher, which is particularly important when evaluating interior defensive linemen in this range. Holmes has enough quickness to attack gaps and create pressure from the inside, giving him a pathway to contribute on passing downs rather than simply being a rotational run defender. His ability to generate disruption without requiring a favorable matchup is an important part of his evaluation, and he's excelled so far this year totaling 11 sacks through 10 games for the Red Raiders.

There are still areas to develop, particularly when it comes to consistently anchoring against combination blocks and maintaining his positioning when offenses devote additional attention to him, but that's part of the appeal of finding a player here on Day 2.

For Philly, Holmes would provide another young body who can compete for snaps in the middle of the defensive line. Established pieces are present, but continuing to add depth and competition to the rotation is important, particularly after moving Davis. Holmes has the ability to contribute against the run while offering pass-rush upside, making him an intriguing developmental option who could eventually grow into a larger role.

Day 2: Jordan Hall, Georgia | 6'4″, 320 lbs, 35 ¼ arm (verified measurements)

The Bulldogs have consistently produced NFL-caliber defensive linemen, and Hall is another player whose combination of size, length, and physicality makes him an intriguing prospect in the class.

The bottom line for the Georgia product – he's an immovable force in the ground game.

There's a level of natural strength to his game that makes it extremely difficult for offensive linemen to create movement against him. He has the length to control a rep early on, size to occupy space, the lower-body strength to anchor against double teams and the physicality to make life difficult for opposing offensive lines attempting to establish their running game. He's not necessarily going to win every snap by making a spectacular play in the backfield, but his ability to screw his cleats into the grass and prevent offenses from creating movement at the point of attack is what makes him so appealing.

And sometimes, that's exactly what a defense needs.

There's also a natural fit when considering the Eagles' recent investment in Georgia defenders. Roseman has consistently shown an appreciation for the program's defensive talent, and Hall would give the Eagles another opportunity to bring in a player from a pipeline they know well.

After moving Davis, adding another big-bodied presence who can control the middle of the defensive line would make plenty of sense. Carter and Ojomo offer different skill sets, and Hall could complement those players by providing a more traditional interior run defender who can eat up space and make it difficult for opposing offenses to establish their identity on the ground.

Day 3: Jide Abasiri, USC | 6'4″, 281 lbs (verified measurements)

I hope Abasiri ends up at an All-Star game in the pre-draft process for another evaluation exposure as he could end up being one of the more intriguing value selections in the middle to late stages of the draft.

A young prospect who just turned 21 years old, Abasiri has all the physical tools NFL teams drool over when going through the evaluation process. At 6-foot-4 and 285 pounds, he brings excellent length to the interior defensive line and has shown enough flashes throughout the season to suggest there's a lot more potential to unlock as he continues developing.

The first thing that stands out is his physical profile, as Abasiri has the type of length that can create immediate problems for opposing offensive linemen, particularly when he's able to extend and establish his hands to keep blockers from getting into his frame. For a younger player, that combination of size and length is an appealing starting point, especially when considering how much development can take place within ideal coaching surroundings in the NFL.

At this stage of the process, consistency is going to be an important part of his evaluation. There's a difference between flashing the ability to win at the college level and consistently executing those techniques against NFL-caliber offensive linemen. Abasiri still has room to develop his overall game, particularly when it comes to translating those physical tools into more consistent production, and it's a main reason why he's in this range to begin with.

Right now, he's someone who could land anywhere from the late third round into the early-mid portion of Day 3, depending on how the rest of the evaluation process unfolds. His age, physical makeup and flashes of disruption give him an appealing developmental profile, and a strong finish to the season followed by a good showing throughout the pre-draft process will absolutely help his stock continue to climb.

For the Eagles, they don't necessarily need every defensive lineman it drafts to come in and immediately command a significant snap share. Finding younger players with legitimate physical upside who can develop behind established veterans is an important part of maintaining a productive defensive front over time, and Abasiri has the length, athleticism and developmental runway to make him an intriguing option later in the draft.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2953. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2951. 3 thoughts as the Mavericks face another winnable matchup at home against the Los Angeles Clippers

The Dallas Mavericks (8-7) remain an interesting case study in the topsy-turvy NBA ahead of Saturday's clash with the Los Angeles Clippers (4-10), and their own penchant for wild swings in performance level are only part of that story.

They've got significant injuries holding them back in some ways, but that comes against the backdrop of players like Zaccharie Risacher and Caleb Martin, who are enjoying various levels of resurgence this year. Kyrie Irving is back and generally playing well, but how back is he really, and how committed are the Mavericks to seeing Irving finish the season with the team? Naji Marshall is playing the role in terms of minutes of a guy who just got extended for three years after setting a career-high scoring mark, but he's averaging just 10.9 points and 2.8 rebounds per game in November, so what gives there? Marshall seems to simply be the lesser evil right now in the mid-off between himself and Max Christie.

The stew is pungent and rich, and it should not be overlooked that, despite the mounting question marks, an 8-7 record is at least one step ahead of schedule as we near the season's quarter pole. The best part about that record is the chance to make it 9-7 against another vulnerable opponent at American Airlines Center on Saturday.

The Clip Show this year is a Darius Garland-Brook Lopez-Rui Hachimura production, with a little Keaton Wagler and Derrick Jones Jr. sprinkled in thus far. The 38-year-old Lopez, in particular, averaging 15.4 points on better than 55% shooting from the floor, is an inspiration for all of us who are past our prime. He's scored 23 or more in three of the Clippers' last four games and 20 or more in five of the last seven. That includes games against the San Antonio Spurs (23) and the Oklahoma City Thunder (26). He' shooting lights out from 3-point range — nearly 45% to start the year.

And Lopez isn't the only Clipper doing work from long range this season. LA is second in the NBA in team 3-point percentage, at 39.2%. Hachimura and Jones Jr. are also hitting about two per game from deep on surprisingly solid shooting clips. Jones' 50% mark on 3.7 attempts per game thus far this year will likely regress, but he's been feeling it from out there to say the least. Max Strus, Kriss Dunn, Gradey Dick and Wagler are all shooters as well.

But yet, they're 4-10. They don't rebound well, they turn the ball over the second most (17.6 per game) in the NBA and they rank 25th in defensive rating (117.2). A tale as old as the peach-basket net, really. Irving and Cooper Flagg should be able to have their way with this bunch, as they did on Wednesday against the flailing Golden State Warriors.

Mavericks first-year head coach Dusty May was asked on Friday if the team is close to having center Dereck Lively II back in action. He gave a very Clintonesque answer, questioning the technical definition of the word "close," which is never a good sign. Yes, Bill, er, Dusty, the whole question does center around what the definition of the word "is" is.

"I asked the same question this morning & close is a term that was used by D-Live," May said, according to the Dallas Morning News. "And when you look at it from the magnitude that he's been out for a while. He's doing a lot more now, but close, I'm not sure what close is. I would have to defer to our medical staff on that. He's in great spirits. He's moving well. I don't know what the exact ramp-up and build-up is, but he's progressing."

"I'm not sure what close is," is doing a lot of heavy lifting in that answer. Close is close. Can you put a number on the number of days or weeks away the player is? No? Ok, then it's not close. It's far.

The whole saga serves to weaken the premise that the Mavs' frontcourt was a definite strength coming into the season. The forwards, led by Flagg and a mildly resurgent PJ Washington, are playing well, but the center position remains a revolving door of things thrown against a wall. The ancient Lopez may find himself in a comfortable spot against the Mavs on Saturday as Dallas looks for a stopgap, but the rest of the team can compensate in other matchups.

The Mavericks have done well against 3-point shooters to start the season, with opponents shooting just 33.4% from distance against them through 15 games. That's the fifth-best mark in the league, and before you make the "variance" claim, just know, that includes a game at the Celtics in which Boston made 23-of-55 (42%) and at the Timberwolves in which Minnesota canned 15-of-32 (47%).

There appears to be something to the Mavs' ability to close out on shooters. If Dallas can limit the Clippers' production from deep, the Mavericks have the ability to make quick work of a Los Angeles side that seems to lack identity above all else.

The Mavericks and the Clippers tip off from American Airlines Center shortly after 7 p.m. on Saturday. Catch the game locally on KFAA Channel 29 or on a sister station throughout the team's regional viewership area. The stream will be on MavsTV or NBA League pass where available. The radio broadcast will be on KRLD 105.3 The Fan.

#2948. ‘What's different? My confidence': Joe Milton used bye week to master offense

'What's different? My confidence': Joe Milton used bye week to master offense originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The bye week gave Joe Milton a chance to do stuff he really had to do.

Milton made his first NFL start after just 23 days and 10 practices with the Eagles and it went about as expected in a 19-11 loss to the Giants, the Eagles' seventh straight loss.

Two weeks later, Milton feels like a different man.

Thanks to the bye week, he's had two weeks to devote to learning an offense that he just didn't have time to learn before his first start.

It's still not a lot of time, but 37 days is a lot different than 23 days.

"What's different for me now? My confidence," Milton said after the Eagles returned to practice Wednesday. "Just confidence in knowing this offense and what these coaches and my teammates around me are instilling in me and talking to me about.

"And just my reason to ask some questions. I'm great at asking questions, and I ask Parks (Frazier) and Sean (Mannion) a lot of questions. And I don't think I can ask (more) questions than I already do. I think I do a great job at that. So just yeah, just my confidence for sure.

"I feel more comfortable than I did last week for sure. Just the verbiage and everything that we needed to do … to solve the problem and fix it."

Milton wasn't awful in the Giants loss considering the circumstances. He didn't have DeVonta Smith or Dontayvion Wicks and the offensive line was unable to protect him, on top of his lack of reps in Mannion's offense.

He'll make his second career start Sunday at the Linc against the Steelers, who are 6-3 but ranked 30th in pass defense, worse even than the Eagles.

For the record, the last Eagles quarterback to lose his first two career starts was Nick Foles in 2012.

For Milton, the bye week couldn't have come at a better time.

"It was very helpful just because I was able to communicate (with) the coaches (about) what we need to do better and what we need to get better at and where I need to be better and just echoing that and fixing the protections for myself. Learning it, studying it more, being able to pick up the blitzes whenever they show up, and just really just understanding the verbiage that we're pronouncing and saying here."

Milton completed 18 for 34 passes for 169 yards against the Giants with a long gainer of 20 yards, no touchdowns or interceptions. He also ran nine times for 64 yards and ran for a two-point conversion. The Eagles are last in the NFL in total offense and 30th in passing offense and scoring.

One thing Milton did during the bye was ask tons of questions. That's big for him, a good way for him to learn. He's big on questions.

"I ask questions every day," he said. "It could be the same question. I just want to make sure I heard correctly and I took down the right note. I'm big on writing notes. And my coaches do a great job of just like overly talking, just constantly mentioning the same thing. And I mean, you kind of hear it every day and all throughout the week, so it kind of gets stuck in your brain."

"Just what do I need to do on this play?" he said. "Like, what is what is the quarterback job specifically on this play? That goes for every play. Like, what do you exactly want me to do on this play? And if there's anything different, just tell me now."

Milton will have Smith and Wicks back Sunday, so that should help.

"Those two guys coming back obviously helps out the team," he said. "For me to throw to those guys today was amazing. I mean, shoot, I couldn't stop smiling for real if you ask me."

The Eagles haven't won a game in two months so there hasn't been a lot of smiling going on around the team facility.

A more settled, more prepared Milton could go a long way toward ending this ugly seven-game losing streak.

"I know what I need to know as far as this offense and whatever goes on in this offense," he said. "So I think I'm in the right place and the right time as far as where I'm at with this offense.

"It's not rocket science. Football is football, no matter where you go. It's all about X's and O's and just knowing football. Coverage is going to be the coverage, a play is going to be a play, and if it's a run game, just hand it off to the running back."

#2946. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#2944. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2941. Roob's bye week stats: It's awful but we do have Makai Lemon and Dallas Goedert

Roob's bye week stats: It's awful but we do have Makai Lemon and Dallas Goedert originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

How bad has it been? We'll document a lot of it in this week's bye-week stats piece, but just to give you an idea … this is the first time in more than half a century the Eagles have played nine games and they don't have a 350-yard rusher, a 350-yard receiver or a 1,000-yard passer.

Nine games in, their leading rusher is Saquon Barkley with 296 yards, their leading receiver is Makai Lemon with 312 yards and their leading passer is Jalen Hurts with 933 yards.

Last time that happened was 1971, when Pete Liske had thrown for 981 yards, leading rusher Tom Woodeshick had 143 yards and leading receiver leading receiver Ben Hawkins had 349 yards after nine games.

It's not fun documenting just how bad a football team is, but this is our reality right now.

But we'll start with the only positive stats we could find with the Eagles buried on this seven-game losing streak.

DALLAS KEEPS PRODUCING: Dallas Goedert has 31 catches this year and has moved up from eighth to third in career receptions among tight ends who've never made a Pro Bowl. The only tight ends in NFL history who caught more passes than Goedert and never made a Pro Bowl are Ben Watson, who had 547 catches from 2004 through 2019, and Hunter Henry, who caught 487 passes and is now with the Patriots in his 10th NFL season. Including postseason, Goedert has 496 career receptions and needs four to become the 26th tight end ever with 500.

THE EAGLES FOUND ONE: With 37 catches on 54 targets, Makai Lemon has the 9th-highest catch percentage (68.5) of 33 wide receivers with at least 50 targets. Among the eight guys he trails are Puka Nacua, Ja'Marr Chase, Jaxon Smith-Njigba and Amon-Ra St. Brown. That 68.5 figure is highest on record by an Eagles rookie through his first nine career games – Stathead traks catch percentage since 1977. Previous high was Keith Jackson's 64.6 percent in 1988 and previous high by a WR was Jordan Matthews with 63.9 in 2014. Highest catch percentage in Eagles history was LeSean McCoy at 78.5. Top WR is DeVonta Smith at 69.1. Lemon's 25 catches over the last five games are the most by an Eagles rookie in any five-game span since Keith Jackson had 36 in 1988 in the second through sixth games of his career. Jackson's 38 catches in his first six games, most ever by a tight end in his first six career games.

HOW CAN YOU ONLY HAVE 5 TAKEAWAYS? The Eagles' five takeaways is their fewest after nine games since 1937, when they also had five. They had one in 1935 and four in 1936, and those are the only other seasons they've had five or fewer. Only four teams have ever had fewer takeaways through nine games since 1940 – the 2011 Steelers (4), 2014 Jets (3), the 2019 Falcons (4) and 2025 Jets (1). Their three interceptions equals the fewest they've ever had after nine games. They also had three in 1998 and 2020. Their streak of four consecutive games without an INT is 4th-longest in franchise history.

THEY DON'T SCORE MUCH: The Eagles have scored 21 or fewer points in all seven games during their losing streak, their longest streak of games without scoring more than 21 points since 1999. It's the first time since 2012 they've scored 24 or fewer in each of their first nine games. They've lost four in a row by eight or more points for the first time since 2012 and only the third time in the last 50 years. They've been outscored this year by 73 points, the 2nd-biggest scoring margin against them through nine games since 1972. They were outscored by 116 the first nine games of 1998. Their 148 points are their fewest after nine games since 1999, when they had 114.

NICE PASSING GAME: The Eagles' 172 passing yards per game are their fewest through nine games since 2003, when they averaged just 151 through eight games. They have had fewer than 210 or fewer passing yards in seven of their nine games, matching their most games under 210 passing yards through nine games since 1979. Their 797 rushing yards are their fewest after nine games since 2005.

BLOWN LEADS: The Eagles led the Commanders 10-3 after the first quarter and led the Giants 8-6 but lost both games. This is the first time in 31 years the Eagles have lost consecutive games that they led going into the second quarter. In 1995, they led the Chargers 7-0 after the first quarter at the Vet before losing 27-21 on a Junior Seau touchdown return after recovering a Calvin Williams fumble and an 88-yard punt return TD by Andre Coleman and a week later they led the Raiders 17-0 after the first quarter before losing 48-17 in what turned out to be Randall Cunningham's final start in an Eagles uniform.

WORST SINCE 1974: Through nine games, the Eagles are averaging 5.7 yards per pass play and 3.7 yards per rushing play. This is the first time they've been that bad both throwing and running the football nine games into a season since 1974 – when they were at 5.5 and 3.3 – and only the second time since 1941. They're the first NFL team as low as 5.7 and 3.7 in the same season through nine games since the 2014 Raiders (5.5, 3.7) and only the 12th in the last 40 years. The only team since 1943 to make the playoffs with averages that low both rushing and passing was the 1971 Vikings, who had one of the greatest defenses of all time.

CLOSING IN ON A RECORD NOBODY WANTS: The Eagles have been outgained by at least 20 yards in all seven games during their losing streak. That matches their most consecutive games getting outgained by 20 or more yards since 1976, when it happened eight games in a row. Their all-time high is 10 straight games in 1971, so they're only three games shy of that "record." They've been outgained by 839 yards so far this year, the biggest yardage margin against them since 1999. During the seven-game losing streak, they've been outgained by 912 yards, the biggest margin against them in any seven-game span since 1976. They've been out-rushed by 338 yards, the largest rushing margin against them since 2005 (352) and 3rd-largest since 1985. Their passing yards margin is 501, largest since 1999 (625) and 2nd-largest since 1976.

THEY HAVEN'T WON IN A WHILE: The seven-game losing streak is tied for 9th-longest in franchise history, but three of those streaks came in 1942 or earlier. Since 1943, is tied for 6th-longest in Eagles history. A loss to the Steelers next weekend will give the Eagles an eight-game losing streak, which would be tied for 4th-longest in franchise history and match their longest since an 11-game losing streak in 1969. They also lost eight straight in 2012.

THE BOBBY HOYING STAT: With Jalen Hurts going 2-3, Tanner McKee 0-3 and Joe Milton losing his first start last week, this is the first time in 28 years three different Eagles starting quarterbacks have lost a game in the same season. In 1998, Rodney Peete was 1-3, Bobby Hoying 1-6 and Koy Detmer 1-4. Also happened the year before. In 1997, Peete was 2-1, Hoying was 2-3-1 and Ty Detmer was 2-5. In 1991, they had four: Randall Cunningham (1-0), Jim McMahon (7-3), Jeff Kemp (1-1) and Brad Goebel (0-2).

FIRST TIME EVER: The Eagles are averaging 4.29 yards per play and allowing 5.37 yards per play. This is the first time in franchise history they've gained under 4.3 yards per play and allowed over 5.3 yards per play through nine games. The 4.29 is their worst on offense since 1999.

THREE STRAIGHT IN THE DIVISION: With consecutive losses to the Cowboys, Commanders and Giants, this is the first time the Eagles have lost division games three weeks in a row in 27 years. Last time it happened was Weeks 12 through 14 in 1999, when they lost to Washington 20-17 in overtime at FedEx, 21-17 to the Cards at Sun Devil Stadium and 33-18 to the Giants at the Vet. The Eagles lost five NFC East games from 2022 through 2024. They lost three in 21 days.

A WENDELL SMALLWOOD SIGHTING: With Saquon Barkley leading the team with 296 rushing yards, this is only the second time since 1992 the Eagles haven't had a 300-yard rusher through nine games. In 2018 their leading rusher after nine games was Wendell Smallwood with 251 yards. Last time before that they didn't have a 300-yard rusher after nine games was 1991, when Heath Sherman had a team-high 244 yards.

OK, THIS IS REALLY BAD: The Eagles have been out-rushed by 338 yards, the largest rushing margin against them since 2005 (352) and 3rd-largest since 1985. Their passing yards margin is 501, largest since 1999 (625) and 2nd-largest since 1976.

#2939. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2935. Eagles-Steelers matchups to watch in Week 11

Eagles-Steelers matchups to watch in Week 11 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles (2-7) are hosting the Pittsburgh Steelers (6-3) at the Linc on Sunday afternoon at 1.

The biggest move Howie Roseman made before the trade deadline was sending Jordan Davis to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a first-rounder and a second-rounder. So this will be the Eagles' first game without their run-stuffing nose tackle and they're facing a team that is averaging 4.4 yards per carry on the ground — the fourth-highest average in the NFL.

The Steelers are 14th in overall rushing but have been efficient this season. Their ground game starts with running back Jaylen Warren, who has 126 carries for 719 yards and 2 touchdowns this season. That 5.7 yards-per-carry average is the best in the NFL among qualified players. Of Warren's 719 yards this season, 533 of them have come after contact. He's averaging 4.2 yards after contact per attempt, which is the fourth-highest average in the NFL. So it'll be important for the Eagles to wrap up in this game.

The good news for the Eagles is that they had their best run defense game of the season before the bye week against the Giants, allowing just 80 yards on the ground. It was the first time all season they held their opponent under 100 rushing yards.

Eagles LBs and S vs. Pat Freiermuth, Darnell Washington

The Steelers' leading receiver this season is DK Metcalf, who has 432 yards and 2 touchdowns on 35 catches. He is also leading the team in targets with 70. So maybe this is another good week to have Quinyon Mitchell travel with a top receiver. But the Steelers also have two tight ends who each see a ton of action in Pat Freiermuth and Darnell Washington.

Freiermuth: 38 targets, 27 catches, 270 yards, 5 TDs

Washington: 31 targets, 20 catches, 259 yards, 3 TDs

The Steelers have 15 passing touchdowns this season and 8 of them come from these two players. So the Eagles have to be aware of them in the red zone.

The Eagles have actually been very good against tight ends this season. They have allowed 38 catches for 386 yards and 3 touchdowns to opposing tight ends this season. Just two teams (Titans and Jaguars) have allowed fewer yards to opposing tight ends. But this will be a big test for their safeties and linebackers, especially if Zack Baun (back) is unable to play.

Aaron Rodgers is one of the greatest QBs in NFL history but he's obviously past his prime. Rodgers will turn 43 on Dec. 2, but he's having an OK season. He has completed 59.9% of his passes for 2,014 yards with 15 touchdowns and 6 interceptions. But he has already been sacked 26 times in nine games. The only QBs who have been sacked more this year are Deshaun Watson (37), Justin Herbert (33), Geno Smith (29) and Bryce Young (29).

Despite that, the Steelers are allowing a pressure rate of just 31.4%, which is the seventh-best QB pressure rate in the NFL. The 49ers are tops at 27.9%. While Rodgers is getting sacked quite a bit, he's also getting the ball out quickly. His average time to throw is 2.75 seconds, which is eighth in the NFL.

The Steelers are going to be without starting left guard Mason McCormick, who suffered an ankle injury in the loss to the Bengals.

The Eagles' defense finally broke out before the bye week with six sacks against the Giants after having just 10 entering the game. But with 16 sacks, they still rank 28th in the NFL this season and their pressure rate of 32.6% ranks 26th.

The plan this season was to have third-round pick Markel Bell sit and learn behind all-time great Lane Johnson but that plan got thrown into a blender earlier this year when Johnson abruptly retired. While Bell has had some rough moments, it'll be his job for the rest of the season. That became abundantly clear when the Eagles released Fred Johnson late last week. In six games, Bell has given up 5 sacks and 19 pressures. There have been ups and downs as the Miami product adjusts to the NFL.

This won't be an easy week. T.J. Watt leads the Steelers with 7 1/2 sacks and is second in pressures on the team with 23. (Don't sleep on Alex Highsmith or Nick Herbig; this is a dangerous group of edge rushers.) Watt, 32, is a future Hall of Famer and a former Defensive Player of the Year who is fourth on the active sack list with 122.5. He's behind just Von Miller (139.5), Cameron Jordan (132) and Myles Garrett (128.5). Oh yeah, and he plays most of his snaps on the defensive left, which sets up this matchup against Bell. The Eagles will need to help the rookie in this game.

Before the bye week, Saquon Barkley was dealing with hamstring and back injuries that were limiting him. Barkley looked good in limited action against the Cowboys but the last two games had 26 carries for 52 yards. Barkley has looked good when healthy this season but he's also running behind an offensive line that has really struggled this season. He'll see a tough test in Week 11.

The Steelers this season have the NFL's No. 3-ranked run defense. They have given up just 83.2 yards per game on the ground in 2026 and have held opponents to 43.3 rushing yards per game over their last three games. They have given up just 0.71 yards before contact per attempt.

DeVonta Smith vs. Asante Samuel Jr., Jamel Dean

In the Steelers' Week 10 loss to the Bengals, they had no answer for Jamar Chase, who ended up with 11 catches on 14 targets for 231 yards. Based PFF's data, Chase caught a pass against six different Steelers defenders but did most of his damage against their top two corners:

Chase vs. Samuel Jr.: 4 targets, 2 caches, 96 yards

Chase vs. Dean: 5 targets, 4 catches, 53 yards

The Steelers have been playing Jalen Ramsey at nickel corner and safety. He's no longer a full-time outside cornerback, so we won't see him on Smith in this game. At one point, the Steelers had a cornerback who would have had a better shot against Smith but they were unable to reach a contract agreement with Joey Porter Jr. and traded him to the Cowboys in late September.

Smith has missed the last three games with a hamstring injury but if he's healthy in this one, it could be a big opportunity for him in his first game with starting QB Joe Milton.
