#2933. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2931. Game Preview #17: Wolves vs Grizzlies Thread

Remember when there was a "budding rivalry" between the Minnesota Timberwolves and Memphis Grizzlies? A drama filled first round playoff matchup between future faces of the league, Anthony Edwards and Ja Morant. Karl-Anthony Towns Sr. and Tee Morant became the cutest "Dads of the NBA" bromance. Dillon Brooks, Mike Conley, Kyle Anderson…just so many storylines.

Now these two teams couldn't appear to be any further away from that. The Grizzlies have zero players remaining on their 2022 roster. The Wolves roster just Anthony Edwards and Jaden McDaniels from their respective squad. The connection these two franchises share is more about who gets to join the Eastern Conference in a few years. In the meantime, contrary to what many thought, Memphis is standing right above Minnesota in the Western Conference standings!

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (11-5) vs. Memphis Grizzlies (12-4)

When: Saturday, November 21 at 7:00 PM CT

If you haven't been paying attention, the Memphis Grizzlies have been one of the feel-good stories of the NBA so far. They completely, and rightfully, ditched their previous oft-injured core of Ja Morant, Jaren Jackson Jr., and Desmond Bane. Many pundits believed that Memphis would be among the worst teams in the league as they rebuild from the ground floor this year (not me, as I was wise beyond comprehension).

Instead, they were shot out of a cannon to begin the season, starting 6-0. No, I'm not talking about a 6-0 scoring run. I'm talking about a 6-0 team record. They follow Tommy Ice/T.I.'s (head coach Tuomas Iisalo) lead, employing a strategy where he spreads minutes across a 10 to 12-man rotation. 11 different players average over 15 minutes per game. They're led by early season MVP candidate Ty Jerome (missed previous four games, probable to return Saturday) and 2026 third overall draft pick, Cameron Boozer (ruled out with ankle injury Saturday).

They fell back to Earth by losing four of their next five games, but have already bounced back to win five straight (against admittedly relatively easy competition). When you zoom out further, the only "good teams" they've beaten are the Trail Blazers and Cavaliers. They've had the easiest strength of schedule by a considerable margin.

So are they for real? More real than most thought.

Equal opportunity to all. That might be on the Grizzlies' whiteboard before every game. 11 different players average over 5.0 field goal attempts per game, compared to just eight for Minnesota. Nine different players average 9.0+ points per game, compared to just seven for Minnesota.

You cannot just guard one player to stop Memphis.

For Minnesota, this means their league-best defense will need to be on a string. Everyone is dangerous and they have to be treated as such. As a team, the Grizzlies make the third most amount of triples per game (15.6) and drain them at a league-best rate (39.6%) – most of these being open attempts. They are third in assists per game. They will spread things around and make themselves unpredictable.

Don't be surprised if a Cam Spencer or Javon Small pop off for 16 points. It's very possible Jerami Grant has a MPE (medium player explosion) and suddenly scores 24 points out of nowhere. Where did Taylor Hendricks come from, and why is he so buff and athletic now? Memphis also has no shortage of quality role players like Cedric Coward and Jaylen Wells, who will muck the game up on both ends of the court.

What has happened to LaMelo Ball's offense? (Did you see what I did? I removed the "O" from his name above. Like he's playing so bad offensively he doesn't have "O" in his name. Do you get it?) After I spent some time developing (more) creative headlines so that I could gush about his defense, it's been quite jarring to see his drop-off in what's supposed to be his main talent.

This month of November has been a seesaw of production so far. If you compare the numbers in the first five games of the month when the Wolves went 5-0, to his most recent four game stretch, it's eye-popping.

If you've been watching the games, you'll see that Ball is clearly trying to shoot his way through this slump. In fact, he's taking three more shots per game during this most recent stretch. Is this something more than just a rough patch? LaMelo had some tape around his left hand which he tugged at during throughout Thursday's game. There hasn't been any reports of ailments, at least not to my knowledge.

We're not at Rudy Gobert levels of offensive concern yet, but I'm keeping me eye on this.

When's the last time the Wolves had a blowout win? Over a week? Yeah, that's too long. It's time for Minnesota to bump up that point differential and pad some stats. LaMelo Ball finally breaks out of his slump and busts up the Grizzlies for a cool 33 points thanks to eight three-pointers. Rudy Gobert gives Cam Boozer his "Welcome the NBA" moment by dunking all over his head off an alley-oop.

#2929. Arnold Ebiketie: No hard feelings after trade to Bengals falls through

Arnold Ebiketie: No hard feelings after trade to Bengals falls through originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Arnold Ebiketie was at breakfast last Tuesday when he got the call.

The Eagles were trading him to the Bengals for a 2027 sixth-round draft pick. As Ebiketie drove home to pack his bags, his mind was racing. He was already thinking about how he was going to ship his car to Cincinnati, pack up his belongings and spend some time with his kids before heading to Ohio. His flight had already been booked.

But about two hours later, Ebiketie got another call. The deal fell through.

"It's the NFL, the business-side of it," Ebiketie said on Wednesday as the Eagles began their preparations to play the Steelers. "I felt like one call I was going to Cincinnati, another call I was staying here. I wasn't mad at the fact that I was staying so I got back to my bye week. That was the best part about it."

The Bengals were looking for some added edge rusher depth before the deadline when they agreed to the trade with the Eagles, but they somehow overlooked the fact that trading for Ebiketie would cancel out a projected third-round compensatory pick coming their way in next year's draft. Ebiketie might have been worth a sixth-round pick, but he wasn't worth losing a third-rounder too.

So the Bengals had to embarrassingly call off the deal and the Eagles had to welcome back a player they very obviously tried to trade.

You would understand if Ebiketie found the situation a little awkward, but he claims it isn't.

"Nah. A lot of people ask me but I wouldn't say it makes me feel awkward," he said. "It's football. Now I'm back at work, I've got to get ready to play Pittsburgh this weekend. It's been normal."

Ebiketie said the first thing he did when he learned about the trade was to look up the Bengals' schedule. He was hoping that Cincinnati hadn't yet had their bye week and was disappointed to learn they already had it in Week 6.

Had the trade gone through, Ebiketie would have had to forfeit a bye week and join the Bengals immediately.

"Getting traded on your bye week, that's not the best thing to happen," Ebiketie said. "But then I realized I was staying, I was like, 'You know what? I can get back to my plans,' which I wasn't doing much. Going to DC for the weekend. Got back to my plans."

The Eagles signed the 27-year-old Ebiketie to a one-year deal this offseason and he has played in all nine games this season. Ebiketie has played a total of 300 defensive snaps (48%) but has yet to register a sack and has just two quarterback hits.

After four years with the Falcons, Ebiketie had to take a one-year, prove-it deal in Philly and he hasn't been very successful. The team hasn't been very successful either.

But Ebiketie is happy to still be in Philly. He's especially happy to have more time with his teammates.

"That's the hardest part, you know," Ebiketie said. "When you make the connections with the guys and you get traded and you have to leave and that's the first thing that goes through your head. The people that you're around in the locker room, people that you're out there playing with, you're definitely going to miss them."

#2927. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2923. Eagles notebook: Nolan Smith reacts to the Jordan Davis trade

Eagles notebook: Nolan Smith reacts to the Jordan Davis trade originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Nolan Smith and Jordan Davis were teammates at Georgia and then became teammates again in the NFL. They have been friends for a long time.

"Man, he's a great guy," Smith said on Friday. "That's part of the business. That's how it goes. I'm pretty sure Jacksonville is picking up a good player. But we have to focus on us. We have leaders in this locker room. We've got guys who can lead and push guys."

Davis was traded to the Jaguars ahead of the trade deadline last week and this was the first chance to catch up with Smith since the trade. So it has been over a week since it all went down. Jalen Carter, who also played with Davis at Georgia before becoming teammates in the NFL, was again not available to reporters this week.

The return on the Davis trade for the Eagles was undeniable. Getting a first-round pick and second-round pick for Davis represented incredible value. But it doesn't mean they won't miss Davis.

"Man, it was special," Smith said of their time together in Philly. "But that time come and go, man. I'm still playing with guys that I played with at Georgia. But that was a real special moment, though."

The Eagles are back from their bye week and Sean Mannion is still their offensive coordinator and play-caller. Mannion this week even said he got a vote of confidence from Nick Sirianni.

"We're in conversation all the time," Mannion said. "Nick's been totally supportive since I got here in, what was it? I guess February, January. We remain in lockstep and it's great getting to work with him every day.

"Like I said, it's the same things I'm saying about the joy I get working with the players in here. The same can be said about our coaching staff, whether it be with Nick, with our assistants, everybody across the board, it's been totally positive."

If Sirianni decided to hand over play-calling to a different coach during the bye week, it would have been understandable. After all, the Eagles are 32nd out of 32 teams in yards this season and are 30th in points. But this is Mannion's offense and going to someone else (like Josh Grizzard) likely wouldn't fix the problems.

Mannion, 34, is new at this. It's his first year as an NFL offensive coordinator and his first time as a play caller. There was always likely going to be a learning curve; but it has been steeper than many envisioned.

But the fact that Sirianni has been asked multiple times this season if he's sticking with Mannion is telling. A lot of the outside criticisms of Mannion are warranted. Mannion tries to block out that discussion.

"I don't pay attention to any of that stuff," Mannion said. "I'm just totally focused on the role I'm in and doing everything I possibly can to help our players be successful, help our team be successful. You mentioned it, but just kind of blocking out the outside noise. I'm doing everything I can every day to help the Eagles and to help us win and be a successful offense. If there's things that are kind of orbiting outside that, it doesn't pertain to that task and that focus that I have on what's going on here."

Eagles quarterback Joe Milton is known for his strong right arm, but he's not a fan of a nickname that has been attacked to him since college: Bazooka Joe.

He prefers the nickname Mr. Rocket, "because bazookas only travel so far."

With this in mind, let's take a look at some nicknames for other Eagles players in 2026. We got some help from ProFootballReference and the Eagles' media guide for this:

Saquon Barkley: SaQuads, ShayshawnZack Baun: The Bald EagleMarkel Bell: The Liberty BellCartavious "Tank" Bigsby: Tank, Bam}Jalen Carter: Baby Rhino, BreadmanBritain Covey: Crash Test CoveyLandon Dickerson: Big CountryJake Elliott: Jake the MakeTanner McKee: The Mormon MissileQuinyon Mitchell: Quinyonamo BayMoro Ojomo: Coach MoTy Robinson: Vanilla GorillaDeVonta Smith: Smitty, Slim ReaperJeremiah Trotter Jr.: Axe Man Jr.Dontayvion Wicks: Wicked, John WickRiq Woolen: RiqtheFreak

Saquon Barkley made it clear that he doesn't know exactly what T.J. Paganetti's role is going to be with the coaching staff now that Paganetti was brought back as a consultant during the bye week.

But Barkley on Friday realized he never really publicly praised Paganetti for his contributions during Barkley's 2,000-yard season. So he took the chance to do that.

"I think having T.J. back is great," Barkley said. "I don't really know to the extent of how involved he's going to be. I'll leave that up to the coaches. I will say in '24. I'm not saying what happened in '24 is going to happen (again). I'm just saying, giving him props. In '24 he probably was like the secret sauce that no one really knew or talked about. He played a key role in the way we were able to play that year and run the ball at a high level.

"Does that mean bringing him in right now that we're going to …? I can't speak on that. To be honest, that's not where my mind's going. But also giving credit to him. There's a reason why Kellen took him when he went. Behind the scenes, fans and people know it's a lot of work that was put in but he definitely played a major part. So it was just good to see his face and have a conversation with him."

Vic Fangio has been coaching in the NFL for decades and is known for his sometimes gruff demeanor. But even he has a soft spot for the recently traded Jordan Davis.

"Yeah, it's just part of the NFL business," Fangio said. "We're going to miss Jordan. I'm going to miss him personally. Got a little emotional with him when we talked about it after it happened.

"I've got a lot of players that over the years I've remained in touch with and had a good relationship with, some 40 years ago, 30, 20, 10 years ago. He's one of them. So, I'll miss him personally, but he'll do good and we'll be ready to roll."

The Eagles late last week waived veteran tackle Fred Johnson, who played an important role with the team for several years.

From 2023-26, Johnson played in 58 games with 14 starts. He resurrected his career in Philly, which led to this unforgettable moment in the 2024 season when Johnson had to enter the game for Lane Johnson in a 15-12 win in the Superdome.

This was Johnson's first real playing time since the 2021 season. And all of his teammates were thrilled for him.

"After that touchdown to Saquon, I almost cried on the sideline," Johnson said. "I ain't going to lie. I got emotional. I got really emotional because I thought I'd never be back in action. Everybody knows some of my story. It's just been a long time coming. When I seen him running to score, I got hype, real hype. I was just grateful for the moment. I'm ready to go back out and do it again."

Here are some streaks to keep in mind as the Eagles go into this game on Sunday:

• After winning their first two games of the season, the Eagles have lost seven straight. That's the longest active losing streak in the NFL after the Chargers were able to get a win on Monday Night Football. With a loss on Sunday, this would be the longest Eagles losing streak since they lost eight in a row in 2012.

• The Eagles have been home underdogs 10 times during Nick Sirianni's time as Eagles head coach. They are 0-10 in those games and have covered just once. The Eagles enter this one as 4.5-point underdogs against the Steelers.

• Under Sirianni, the Eagles have been very good coming off the bye week. They are 5-0 in those games. They are one of three teams to be undefeated off bye weeks since 2021, along with the Ravens (6-0) and Bills (5-0).

• The Steelers last road win against the Eagles came at Franklin Field on Oct. 24, 1965. Since then, the Eagles have won 11 straight home games against the Steelers — their last win at home against the Steelers came in December of 2024. Here's where those games took place:

Franklin Field: 4Veterans Stadium: 3Lincoln Financial Field: 4

There's a chance this is something that interests only me. But sometimes when there aren't a ton of obvious questions for Michael Clay, I like to get into the weeds with some special teams topics. This week, I asked him about how the Eagles coach the defense in situations where they stay on the field for a punt return.

A couple of these have come up this year. One happened against the Giants in Week 9 when the defense stayed on the field for a 4th-and-1 in the first quarter. Riq Woolen — not normally in as a jammer — ended up getting called for an illegal block.

Here was Clay's answer about getting the defense ready for those situations:

"I talk about it a lot on Fridays in our meetings right here. Obviously, I have all the defense on Saturdays in our walk-through, where we're able to go through a lot of it and talk about different things. What does this coordinator like to do in fourth-and-short scenarios? Who's their personnel? Is it a running back? Can this punter throw? Just little tidbits in there so they know, hey, alert. Something very similar to last year with Detroit. We knew (Dave) Fipp and Detroit ran a lot of fakes in that 4th-and-2, 4th-and-3 area, but it's just my job to give these guys the information. Hopefully, they can retain it, and a lot of the time they do, so they keep themselves, one, from getting hurt and, two, alert for something, a team trying to steal an extra possession, especially in a defensive back-and-forth."

#2922. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

The California lawmaker is banking on his party leaders reinstating a panel that would give him a major platform ahead of 2028.

#2915. Jalen Rose admits interest in ‘Inside the NBA’ job after Charles Barkley’s ringing endorsement

Charles Barkley already has an idea of who he wants to replace him on "Inside the NBA" — Jalen Rose.

Rose, the former NBA player and longtime analyst, said Thursday that he would be interested in joining the award-winning studio show, with Barkley's blessing, of course.

"I love Charles, and yes, that would be something that I would be interested in," Rose told Front Office Sports.

Rose, 53, also expressed appreciation for the opportunities he has received to work alongside Barkley and the rest of the show's cast, as the show opens up to other NBA standouts, like Rose and Draymond Green.

"I appreciate my opportunities the last couple of years to not only contribute to Inside the NBA, but work with TNT on college basketball with Adam Lefkoe, Jamal Mashburn and Bruce Pearl," Rose said.

Rose's comments came more than a month after Barkley publicly endorsed Rose as his ideal replacement while on "BetMGM Gameday Live."

Shaquille O'Neal, Ernie Johnson, Jalen Rose, Kenny Smith and Charles Barkley talk before the game between the New York Knicks and the Indiana Pacers during Game 6 of the 2025 Eastern Conference Finals on May 31, 2025 at Gainbridge Fieldhouse in Indianapolis, Indiana. NBAE via Getty Images

"I think Draymond would do a good job," Barkley said in early October. "I like Draymond, but Jalen Rose is fantastic. He's substituted for us sometimes, Jalen Rose is unbelievable, Vince Carter is unbelievable… but Jalen Rose to me, would be the perfect guy because he's done the most with us."

Rose, who spent 16 years at ESPN before being a victim of the network's 2023 layoffs, occasionally filled in on "Inside the NBA" and other basketball programming on TNT, the show's broadcast home until 2026.

He was greatly appreciative of Barkley's endorsement.

Jalen Rose attends the 16th Annual Jalen Rose Leadership Academy Celebrity Golf Classic Kick-Off Pairings Party in Detroit, Michigan, on July 26, 2026. Getty Images for Operation Gradu

"The one thing I learned about this business is everything isn't transactional," Rose said. "If Charles Barkley is going to say into a microphone that you're the person he feels should replace him, you can't write a check for that, even if it never happens. Basically, what [Barkley is saying] is, 'I'm running one lane in one race, and I'm considered the GOAT to a lot of people, but there's somebody else that I feel like, you know what?

"Y'all should put some respect on his name.'"

#2912. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2910. Moritz Wagner’s first-ever chance to face his brother Franz is nothing short of ‘awkward’

Brooklyn's Moritz Wagner has been in the NBA for nine years and alive for 20 more, and he had never, ever played against his brother, Franz, in a game that counted.

That is, until Friday night. The Wagner siblings faced off when the Nets hosted Franz's Orlando Magic, and they squared off against each other for the first time.

"It's awkward, I'm not gonna lie. I don't really know how to compartmentalize it, really deal with it, and try to focus on the game plan and see what happens emotionally," Moritz said beforehand. "It's a dilemma because you want him to do well. At the same time, you game plan for him, you prepare to limit him. So, it's an awkward feeling. I've never been in this position.

Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner (13) warms up before an NBA basketball game against the Orlando Magic at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, NY, Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

"It's weird. It's definitely a new role I've got to grow into. But it's also fun, and we talked about that [Thursday]. It's an absolute privilege, and for our family it's really cool because we've established each other as independent NBA players on two different sides and get to play against each other in this league and have 20 people here watching us. So it's very special, and it's gonna be an emotional night for our family."

Moritz played for Michigan and was a 2018 first-round pick by Orlando, where he'd spent his entire career until signing with the Nets this summer.

Franz, four years his junior, had followed in his footsteps and starred for the Wolverines before being picked eighth overall by the Magic in 2021.

Orlando Magic's Franz Wagner handles the ball during an NBA Cup game against the Brooklyn Nets at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, N.Y., Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him," Franz said. "But as weird as it's going to be, it's a blessing to share the court with your family.Not a lot of people get to do that, so that's how I look at it."

The younger Wagner is breaking out this season with Paolo Banchero injured, entering the meeting averaging 24.4 points, 8.5 assists and 6.9 rebounds.

And it was 30.7 points, 8.5 assists and 7.7 boards over his prior six games.

"We've got a new staff, a new system and a lot of clarity on roles and what's expected of everyone. That helps everyone with their rhythm," Franz said.

"He's playing incredible," Moritz said. "New coach, new system, found a way to be a playmaker and scorer at the same time, dominating the pace, the paint. Obviously with an enormous usage rate as well, which you've got to give him credit for the physicality he plays with. One thing that's really underrated about Franz is his defensive ability. There aren't many players that dominate on both ends of the floor like he does. … It's a joy to watch him as a big brother, regardless of my situation."

Young fan Dylan wears a custom jersey featuring Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner and Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner in Brooklyn, N.Y., Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

That situation is serving as backup center and veteran voice on a struggling Nets squad.

Many have suggested that Brooklyn could target Franz if Orlando breaks him and Banchero up. But for the moment, the Nets have to figure out their own woes internally, and Franz was the enemy Friday.

"I grew up with him. When he's mad, when I see that look in his eyes, it takes me back to when we were 9 and 5 and playing against each other. I don't think I know a human better than him. So on an emotional level, just knowing what he goes through with every step, it's weird to be the opponent," Moritz said.

"Obviously, [he's] a big part of my development as a person and as a player," Franz said. "So it should be fun out there."

Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner (22) warms up before a game against the Brooklyn Nets on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, N.Y. Robert Sabo for NY Post

The Wagner family was on hand to watch Friday. They even had specialized jerseys made with both of the brothers' names and team colors, and their mother Beate is especially nervous.

"We made these corny split jerseys," Moritz said. "I know my mom's going to go through it [Friday]. I know that. … That's going to be really difficult for her. Definitely a new spot for her. … But she's going to figure that out, and it's also a really good problem to have, obviously.."

#2908. Jaguars saw something in Liam Coen that other teams didn't

In a season and a half with the Jaguars, Liam Coen has established himself as one of the best coaches in the NFL. Last year, Coen took over a team that had gone 4-13 the year before and improved their record to 13-4 — the best record ever for a first-year coach taking over a team that had won four or fewer games the year before. This year Coen is showing that last year was no fluke, with the Jaguars at 7-2 with an NFL-best point differential of +125.

Coen's great success should have other teams that hired new head coaches in 2025 asking themselves: What did we miss? Because the Jaguars were so high on Coen that he was their No. 1 target and they were willing to do whatever it took to get him, while no other team even bothered to give Coen an interview.

During the 2025 NFL coaching search, we tracked dozens of reports of head coaching interviews and requests among the seven teams that had vacancies. The Jaguars were the only team that so much as asked Coen to interview.

This week there's been some media scrutiny on the Jets, who hired Aaron Glenn without considering Coen as a candidate. Glenn is 5-22; Coen is 20-6. But it's unfair to single out the Jets for failing to interview Coen, because no team other than the Jaguars did, either.

The 41-year-old Coen had a résumé that should have made him a good candidate. NFL teams area always on the lookout for coaches who can nurture a franchise quarterback and run an innovative offense. Coen played quarterback at UMass and coached quarterbacks in college before getting hired by Sean McVay to work on the Rams' staff, eventually became the Rams' offensive coordinator under McVay, and had been a successful playcaller as offensive coordinator for the Buccaneers, as well as at Kentucky. With that background, it's surprising that only the Jaguars wanted him.

And the Jaguars really wanted him, to the point that they were willing to do whatever it took to hire him. Coen initially turned the Jaguars down and agreed to a contract extension to stay with the Buccaneers, but Jaguars owner Shad Khan wouldn't take no for an answer. When Coen said he was concerned about the direction of the Jaguars' front office, Khan fired General Manager Trent Baalke and agreed to reshape the front office in a way that Coen would approve of if Coen would take the job. Coen was persuaded to take the Jaguars job, and the Jaguars later hired James Gladstone, who had worked with Coen on the Rams, as GM.

The result has been the Jaguars going from one of the worst teams in the NFL to one of the best. Coen's success in Jacksonville has justified the faith Khan had in him. And raises questions about the total lack of interest from the rest of the league.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2907. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2906. Celtics could be without two starters against Kings

SACRAMENTO — The Celtics could be without Jayson Tatum and Derrick White when they face the Sacramento Kings on Saturday night.

Tatum is listed as questionable due to rest, while White is listed as questionable with a left AC joint contusion (the acromioclavicular joint connects the collarbone to the top of the shoulder blade).

The Celtics are playing on the first night of a back-to-back; they face the Kings on Saturday at 10pm ET, and then the Golden State Warriors on Sunday at 8:30pm ET.

White has yet to miss any games this season; he's averaging 12.7 points, 5 assists, and 3 rebounds per game, while shooting 42.5% from the field and 30.6% from three.

Tatum has missed one game to rest; he's averaging 26.4 points, 6.8 assists, and 6.6 rebounds, shooting 42.8% from the field and 33.3% from three.

Baylor Scheierman has started two games this season and would be a likely candidate to start in the event that either Tatum or White is sidelined. Scheierman is averaging 5.7 points, 3.7 rebounds, and 1.5 assists in 19.4 minutes per game.

Payton Pritchard, who has yet to start a game this season, could also potentially start. Pritchard is averaging 13.7 points, 3.8 assists, and 3.1 rebounds in 27.3 minutes per game, the fourth-most on the team.

It's also possible that Jordan Walsh will see a reduced role on Saturday; Walsh, who has started 10 of 15 games this year, played less than 5 minutes in Wednesday's loss to the Nuggets.

In that event, Sam Hauser or Hugo Gonzalez could see increased opportunities; Hauser is averaging 8.4 points and 3.1 rebounds in 20.3 minutes per game, while Gonzalez is averaging 3.9 points and 2.5 rebounds in 12.9 minutes per game. Hauser has been lights out from three so far this season, shooting 46.8% from beyond the arc.

When they face the Celtics, the Kings will be playing on the second night of a back-to-back; they face the Los Angeles Lakers on Friday night. The Kings have the second-worst record in the NBA at 1-13.

Celtics-Kings tips off at 10pm ET on Saturday night.

#2905. Can Washington steal the NFC East crown? Hogs Haven readers weigh in after Thursday’s in over the Giants - poll results

Here's what Ben Standig had to say earlier this week:

The 2026 NFL regular season is past the halfway point, but the finish line remains too far away to make sweeping declarations about how the NFC East race will unfold.

Suggesting that the eventual division winner will have at least eight victories doesn't seem like a stretch, in theory.

There's a solid argument that the Washington Commanders are the best all-around team among the remaining contenders. (Step aside, Eagles.)

When Washington sat at 1-3 following a dismal overseas loss to Indianapolis in Week 4, talk of an NFC East title felt like pure fantasy. Yet six games later, the burgundy & gold have quietly stitched together a 3-2-1 stretch highlighted by Thursday night's 24-21 win over the New York Giants at MetLife Stadium that has breathed a sense of renewed optimism into at least part of the fan base.

Now at 3-5-1, Washington still occupies third place in the division, trailing New York by 2 games. Even so, the underlying progress is real. Quarterback Jayden Daniels engineered a clutch late drive last Thursday night — connecting with Stefon Diggs on a 32-yard touchdown with 1:05 remaining — while Washington converted 7 of 14 third downs and held New York to 4-of-12 on third down.

Moreover, over the past 6 games, the Commanders have largely protected the football — carrying a +7 turnover differential on the season while leaning on young contributors like tight end Ben Sinnott, wideout Antonio Williams, and cornerbacks Trey Amos and (the not-so-young) Amik Robertson.

In this week's Hogs Haven Reacts survey, we asked readers two questions to gauge the community's temperature entering Week 11. The first was our weekly benchmark: Are you confident in the overall direction of the team?

This week, 46% of respondents answered "Yes," while 54% voted "No".

That 32-point surge from last week's 14% approval mark mirrors the 21-point jump we saw in Week 9 after Washington beat the Eagles. Nothing cures midweek angst quite like beating a division rival in prime time!

Still, the fact that 54% of voters remain in the "No" camp shows that skepticism runs deep. As several commenters and fans have mentioned, Washington has yet to beat a clear playoff contender outside of Seattle in Week 3, and they let winnable games against the Colts and Giants slip away earlier in the fall. Until the Commanders can achieve sustained winning, a majority of the fan base is keeping its guard up, and the heat will stay turned up on head coach Dan Quinn and GM Adam Peters.

Our second question asked which team respondents believe will win the NFC East division title in 2026. Despite Washington sitting behind both New York and Dallas in the standings, the results were fairly decisive:

That 54% vote represents a dramatic reversal from earlier in the season, but it stems less from sudden perfection in Ashburn than from the vulnerabilities of Washington's rivals.

First, Washington owns the head-to-head tiebreaker over the Giants (5-3-1) after Thursday's win. Second, the schedule-makers did New York no favors. Over the final 8 weeks, the Giants must run a gauntlet that includes Jacksonville, San Francisco, Seattle, and Detroit, whereas Washington gets much softer matchups against Arizona, Tennessee, and Atlanta.

Meanwhile, Dallas (4-6) has been unable to win back-to-back games since November 28, 2025, and its defensive struggles reached a boiling point this week when head coach Brian Schottenheimer stripped first-year coordinator Christian Parker of play-calling duties in favor of senior defensive assistant Mike Pettine. To beat out Washington for the division title, the Cowboys will have to win as many times in their remaining 7 games as the Commanders win in their final 8. Don't forget that the Commanders still have a chance to inflict a loss on the Cowboys when they meet in Landover for the final game of the regular season in Week 18.

The math is straightforward: if Washington can match the win totals of both the Giants and Cowboys, the Commanders will be the division winner!

Washington holds the tiebreaker over the Giants, and, because of the Week 5 tie, matching Dallas in the win column would put Washington a half-game ahead of the silver & blue.

If, for example, the Commanders can win 5 of their final 8 games to finish 8-8-1, they would be division champs if the Giants go 3-5 and the Cowboys, who have only 7 games remaining, finish out the season 4-3. Of course, with 8 weeks to go, there are sure to be a lot of twists & turns before things are decided, but the Cowboys' games in Week 17 vs the Giants and Week 18 on the road against the Commanders could prove to be decisive in determining the ultimate division champ.

Readers in the survey article comment thread broke cleanly along familiar lines.

The optimistic Pollyanna camp — voiced by Savo_UK, Redskins21Life, and BillWard among others– pointed directly to the schedule imbalance. Their consensus is that if Washington takes care of business against Arizona, Tennessee, Atlanta, and Dallas (in Week 18), stealing just 1 upset against Cincinnati, Houston, Minnesota, or Jacksonville would be enough to reach 8 wins, which could be enough to claim the division in this 2026 season.

The skeptical Cassandra camp — led by theFan4Life, WillThomas, and Greengmork among others — warned against mistaking a single prime-time escape against the Giants for a turnaround. They argue that Washington's defense still struggles to generate consistent pressure, that the roster lacks the talent to beat playoff-caliber offenses, and that finishing around .500 risks trapping the franchise in draft-position purgatory without building a true contender.

I think the Commanders defense has looked completely different since the return of Nick Cross and Trey Amos to the lineup.

In the first 5 games of 2026, the Commanders surrendered an average of 29.8 points per game. Since Week 6 in San Francisco, when Amos and Cross took the field together for the first time, Washington has given up 18.5 points per game. While that stretch did include games against the struggling Eagles and last week's road trip to New York, it also included games against the Niners and Rams.

While passing yards surrendered since Week 6 are not dramatically improved (255 per game in Wks 1-5; 232 per game in Wks 6-10), the number of passing TOUCHDOWNS surrendered has changed dramatically, from 2.8 per game (14 total) in the first 5 weeks to 0.75 per game (3 total) in the four most recent games; the secondary has not allowed more than one passing touchdown in any of those contests.

Week 1 at Eagles (L 24–22): 182 net yds (203 gross), 3 TDs

Week 2 at Cowboys (L 37–20): 266 net yds (279 gross), 4 TDs

Week 3 vs. Seahawks (W 33–31): 393 net yds (393 gross), 4 TDs

Week 4 vs. Colts (L 30–13): 129 net yds (143 gross), 0 TDs

Week 5 vs. Giants (T 27–27): 305 net yds (326 gross), 3 TDs

Week 6 at 49ers (L 23–17): 249 net yds (264 gross), 1 TD

Week 8 vs. Eagles (W 27–10): 177 net yds (212 gross), 0 TDs

Week 9 vs. Rams (L 20–13): 219 net yds (228 gross), 1 TD

Week 10 at Giants (W 24–21): 283 net yds (293 gross), 1 TD

Anyone looking at the full-season stats for Washington's pass defense would question the Commanders' ability to limit the Bengals' high-powered attack this week. But a look at the most recent 4 games — which include playing against Matt Stafford & Puka Nacua as well as Brock Purdy and the Kyle Shanahan offense — might well bolster the confidence of Commanders fans with respect to improvements in Washington's pass defense since Cross and Amos took the field together.

If you only look at the scoreboard, it is easy to argue that Washington's offense is still spinning its wheels. To my eye, though, the underlying efficiency and rhythm of David Blough's unit have taken a noticeable step forward over the past month.

Across the four-game stretch straddling the Week 7 bye, the Commanders have pushed their average to 7.6 yards per pass attempt—a marked jump from the 4.8 to 6.3 yards per attempt they managed during the opening month—while completing 66.7 percent of their throws. Even in the Week 9 loss to Los Angeles, Jayden Daniels posted a 101.4 passer rating (24-of-35 for 253 yards) with explosive strikes of 42 yards to Stefon Diggs, 21 yards to Van Jefferson, and a 45-yard burst from Rachaad White, before following that up on Thursday night in New York by going 23-of-33 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 24–21 road win over the Giants.

That progress reflects a deliberate coaching emphasis on sharper situational execution; prior to Week 10, head coach Dan Quinn noted that the staff added extra practice repetitions specifically targeting red-zone spacing against man and zone looks so that "the decision-making process happens quickly" and the offense stops settling for field goals.

Just as encouraging has been the growing trust between Daniels and his first-time play-caller, paired with Daniels' emergence as the unit's vocal leader. There were plenty of justifiable questions early on about Blough's learning curve, yet Daniels emphasized ahead of the Giants game that their weekly game-planning loop is actively tailoring the playbook to his strengths: "I think just stuff I don't like throughout the week, he'll take out. Stuff I really do like, he'll call… If we got all 11 people going in the same direction, we have a chance."

After some early-season failures, that buy-in has shown up recently when it has mattered, most notably during Week 8's 27–10 win over Philadelphia, when Daniels gathered the offense during a sluggish 10–0 start—calling their opening series "complete BS"—and sparked 27 unanswered points.

Rookie wideout Antonio Williams credited Daniels' command in the huddle for uniting the group ("All the guys respect what he has to say, because we know the work he puts in"), while Quinn praised the quarterback's resilience in Thursday night's comeback after overcoming a turnover on downs and two interceptions to engineer the game-winning drive, saying, "To see us battle and stay in the fight, that's what I was looking for."

All in all, I see a team that struggled early and is still finding its feet, but one that is on the rise. The Commanders still haven't put together that full 60-minute game that was the hallmark of the 2024 season, but I believe that they're moving in that direction, and, with 8 games left to play, there's plenty of time to finish putting it together.

Meanwhile, in a different SB Nation poll this week:

Outside the DMV, barely one in six voters (15.7%) wanted a piece of Washington at +350—a stark contrast to the 54% of Hogs Haven readers projecting a division crown, and a gap the Commanders can start closing under the lights on Monday night.

The final 8-game push begins Monday night at Northwest Stadium against Joe Burrow and the AFC North-leading Cincinnati Bengals.

Anyone who watched the last regular-season meeting between these clubs on Monday Night Football in September 2024 remembers a clinic in offensive efficiency. Washington won 38-33 in a game that featured zero punts and zero turnovers — a feat the NFL hadn't seen since 1940.

If Washington wants to get the win again this Monday, the Commanders may need to be ready for another track meet. Burrow has Cincinnati rolling on a five-game winning streak, averaging 30.9 points per game behind Ja'Marr Chase and Tee Higgins. For Washington to pull the upset, pass rushers Dorance Armstrong, Odafe Oweh, and K'Lavon Chaisson will have to disrupt Burrow's timing while the secondary continues to improve, and the Daniels-led offense keeps pace on the scoreboard.

Moneyline: Bengals -270 / Commanders +220

Winning on Monday night is a tall order, although, with Arizona and Tennessee up next, even a loss wouldn't derail Washington's plan for a division title. Still, catching fire under the Monday night lights would likely turn the current cautious optimism into genuine belief across the DMV.

#2904. NBA Star Tari Eason Sues Ex, Claims at Least $92K in Losses Over Missing Rolex and Bleached Designer Clothes

Houston Rockets forward Tari Eason is suing a former girlfriend, claiming at least $92,000 in losses involving his personal belongings.

The 25-year-old's lawsuit names Marilyn Maritza Melo Rodriguez as the defendant. His claim covers both missing possessions and damage to his wardrobe.

According to court documents reported by TMZ on November 19, Eason alleges Rodriguez entered his rental property without permission in June.

Eason says he and his attorney repeatedly asked Rodriguez to return his belongings, but she refused. His complaint seeks their return or financial compensation.

The items identified in the filing include a Rolex watch, Chrome Hearts glasses and a Louis Vuitton duffel bag Eason values at $15,000. He also accuses Rodriguez of pouring bleach over his designer clothing.

The complaint seeks up to three times his claimed losses under California law, along with additional compensation and attorney fees.

Eason petitioned for a domestic violence restraining order in Los Angeles County in July. As Chron reported, he alleged that Rodriguez repeatedly struck him in the face during an argument.

He also claimed that one of her associates called and threatened to break his legs and kill him. Eason said the threats left him fearing for his safety.

A source close to Rodriguez disputed the earlier account in TMZ's July 31 update, describing it as false and misleading. The source said the portrayal was intended to damage her reputation and divert attention from her own allegations of harassment and emotional distress.

A judge granted the temporary restraining order on July 30. Its restrictions barred Rodriguez from approaching Eason, his home, workplace and vehicle, as well as NBA facilities where he plays or practices.

TMZ's latest report said the order remained in effect, with another hearing scheduled for July 29, 2027.

#2903. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

The Jaguars defense will represent the toughest challenge for McCarthy. His first three starts were against the Houston Texans (18th in points allowed per game), the Philadelphia Eagles (19th) and the Washington Commanders (20th). The Jaguars are allowing 16 points per game, second best in the NFL. The Jaguars have been particularly stout against the run, allowing the sixth-fewest rushing yards per game. They rank in the top 10 in yards per carry allowed, rush defense expected points added and success rate on run plays.

The Giants can't dwell on the opponent here, though. They need to stay focused on doing the basic elements correctly and consistently to be the best version of themselves. That's the recipe. "It always is. That's the formula," tight end Chris Manhertz said. "I think it's pretty simple from my vantage point: Take care of the ball, take care of your quarterback and execute at a high level.  Assignment, technique have to be on point. Every man is handling their 1/11th.

"Now it's easier said than done," he added. "There are people who are gonna win [their matchup] and people who are gonna lose. But I tend to believe if we're able to do all of those things on a high level in three phases, the outcome is usually good. So the goal is always to put all three of those together in concert. And if there's a void in one of those phases, the other two phases fill it and pick it up. But to me it's simple."

#2900. Taylor Jenkins Explains Key Thing Bucks Are Missing to Close Games

The Milwaukee Bucks lost their 12th straight game when they fell 124-114 to the Portland Trail Blazers at Fiserv Forum on Thursday.

While riding the league's worst losing streak, that was their first close game in some time. The Bucks trailed by just four points going into the fourth quarter. Inexperience ended up letting them down, but there are positives to be drawn, all things considered.

"Better execution," head coach Taylor Jenkins said afterward when asked what his team needs to do to get back in the win column. "Just our ability to play with pace and play organized. We dialed up what we wanted to do, but we didn't really get in attack mode once we got there.

"We knew where we could create an advantage, and we did, but we couldn't take advantage of it."

The losses hurt, but the manner in which the Bucks lost to the Trail Blazers was received well by their fans.

In the first season of a rebuild under a new coach, it's all about growth and progress, and that was on display despite coming up short.

The biggest positive was the fact that both of Milwaukee's lottery picks from this year's NBA Draft started together and played well.

No. 10 pick, guard Brayden Burries, posted 14 points, three rebounds, three assists, and two steals on 5-for-13 shooting as he continues to cement himself as a starter for Jenkins.

No. 13 pick, forward Nate Ament, notched career highs in points (18) and rebounds (9) in just his second start. The 6-foot-10 forward shot 6-for-11 from the field.

Ament's offensive repertoire is more advanced than Giannis Antetokounmpo's when the Bucks drafted the 'Greek Freak'. However, their measurables are eerily similar, and everyone in Milwaukee is excited about the future of another skinny, long, and athletic big man.

Jenkins is not shying away from playing around with his lineups and rotations, and he might've found something against the Blazers.

Despite the defeat and being down two usual starters, Kyle Kuzma and Myles Turner, the Bucks were entertaining and competitive, with four starters and two bench players scoring in double figures.

Tyler Herro dropped a team-high 19 points, suggesting he may be breaking out of his mini slump. Kel'el Ware added 11 points.

In the second unit, Ryan Rollins returned after missing two games with an illness and posted 15 points, three rebounds, six assists, and four steals.

Second-year guard Kasparas Jakucionis impressed again with 18 points on 6-for-11 shooting. The Bucks acquired the 20-year-old in the Giannis trade with Miami, and he is emerging as a very promising prospect for this rebuild. Jenkins has certainly taken a liking to him.

The Bucks are all but out of playoff contention already at 1-15 — their worst start to a season in franchise history. However, even a good second half of the campaign — laying down the foundations for an improved 2027-28 — would give fans in Milwaukee reasons to rejoice.
