#2899. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2897. What's next for Arch Manning's future as Texas QB partakes in Senior Day?

"Closing Time" by Semisonic may soon be playing in the background for Arch Manning, as the possibility of his college football career coming to an end is looming, though that outcome is far from certain.

One milestone, however, is guaranteed for the Texas quarterback in Week 12: Senior Day.

Manning is set to take part in the senior day festivities ahead of Texas' Week 12 matchup against Arkansas at DKR-Texas Memorial Stadium on Saturday, joining the rest of the Longhorns' senior class as they are recognized for their contributions to the program during their time on the Forty Acres.

College football Fraud Alert: Texas wins, but Arch Manning is guilty

Manning has endured another up-and-down season for the Longhorns with the highs including a come-from-behind win against then-No. 1 Ohio State and wins against Tennessee and Ole Miss, while lows include turning the ball over multiple times in the fourth quarter in back-to-back losses to Oklahoma and Florida.

Despite all that, Manning still holds a strong 18-5 record while serving as Texas' full-time starter, which includes a 9-2 mark against top-ranked opponents.

That all said, there is a possibility of Manning prolonging his career. Once viewed as a near lock for the No. 1 overall pick in the NFL draft, Manning's draft stock is no longer as clear-cut as it once appeared, and he has another year of eligibility remaining, so a return to college football is far from an unrealistic scenario.

Here's what to know about his future plans:

Yes, Manning will partake in Texas' senior day festivities on Saturday ahead of its final home game of the season against Arkansas.

"I'm a senior, so I'm doing it," Manning said earlier this week.

Manning's decision to return to the Longhorns for the 2026 season followed in line with what each of his uncles, Eli and Peyton Manning, and his grandfather, Archie Manning, did by exhausting their eligibility before moving on to play in the NFL. Peyton and Eli Manning were both the No. 1 picks of their respective drafts in 1998 and 2004, while Archie Manning was the No. 2 overall pick in the 1971 NFL Draft.

While Manning is a senior and will be part of senior day on Saturday, it doesn't necessarily mean he'll be moving on from college or the Longhorns after this season. How come? Well, Manning has an extra year of eligibility — as he redshirted his true freshman season after appearing in just three games — so he could return to Texas for another season or transfer somewhere else. Or he could declare for the NFL draft.

Trick Play: Texas pulls off slick trick play against Lane Kiffin, using Arch Manning as decoy

Yes, Manning is eligible for the 2027 NFL Draft, which is the second straight draft he's eligible to declare.

It remains unclear whether Manning will declare after the Longhorns' season for the NFL draft or return for a fifth season. The Longhorns have KJ Lacey Jr. and Dia Bell already on their roster as Manning's backups, and have three-star quarterback Ty Knuster verbally committed to their 2027 recruiting class.

According to NFL Operations, to be eligible for the draft, a player must have been removed from high school for three years to be eligible.

"To be eligible for the draft, players must have been out of high school for at least three years and must have used up their college eligibility before the start of the next college football season. Underclassmen and players who have graduated before using all their college eligibility may request the league's approval to enter the draft early.

"Players are draft-eligible only in the year after the end of their college eligibility."

Manning is listed as the No. 22-ranked quarterback by ESPN's Mel Kiper Jr. in the 2026 NFL Draft class.

Polling NFL mock drafts from several major outlets — such as USA TODAY, CBS Sports and Sports Illustrated The 6-foot-4 quarterback is not in any first-round projections as of right now.

Back in late October, in the midst of a stretch of back-to-back losses to Oklahoma and Florida, an NFL scout told USA TODAY Sports' Matt Hayes that Manning's draft stock hasn't exactly been elevated by his then-19 games as a full-time starter at Texas despite having some notable top-ranked wins on his resume.

"Someone might take him in the third or fourth round right now. But a 1? No chance," the NFL scout told USA TODAY Sports. "There's any number of issues. Decision-making and arm strength at the top of the list."

#2895. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2888. Manhattan wins Class B football title on 2-point stop

WORDEN — Manhattan quarterback Tyson Pavlik scored two touchdowns, one in double overtime, and the Tigers stopped Huntley Project's Blake Ramaeker on a two-point PAT to win their second straight State B football championship Friday, 21-19.

Both teams scored in the first quarter — the 230-pound Pavlik had a 4-yard run at the 4:14 mark and Huntley Project's Tucker Kaczmarek scored on a 16-yard run 3 minutes later — but were shut out for the rest of regulation.

The Tigers (11-1) had a long clock drive at the end, driving from their own 14 to Project's 3-yard line. But with under 10 seconds left Pavlik threw a pass that Brayden Zikmund caught for no gain and Manhattan couldn't get off another play before time ran out.

The Red Devils (12-1) had the ball first in overtime and jumped ahead when Ramaeker broke four tackles on a 9-yard touchdown scramble. Their PAT kick missed, leaving it 13-7.

Manhattan answered with a 10-yard option pass from Zikmund to Matthew Fenno — the Montana State commit catching a TD from the Montana recruit — but then had its PAT kick blocked.

In the second OT — teams start first-and-goal from the 10 — Pavlik scored on fourth down from the 1, then pitched to Easton DeHaan for the successful 2-point PAT and a 21-13 lead.

The Red Devils' Huntly Harp matched Pavlik, scoring on fourth down from the 1. On the PAT, the slippery Ramaeker scrambled left but was wrapped up by Taysen Meech and Zikmund at the 1-yard line.

It was the third football championship and the fourth straight title appearance for Manhattan, which lost to Florence in 2023 and Malta in 2024. Last year the Tigers beat Three Forks, their neighbor 12 miles down I-90, for their second state title. Their first came in 2020.

It was Project's eighth title game appearance and first since a loss to Fairfield in 2015. The Red Devils won State B titles in 1998 and 2008-09.

#2887. Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2884. How China is using summits to expand its global influence

SHENZHEN, China — For a second year in a row, Chinese President Xi Jinping has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores.

U.S. President Donald Trump had a trilateral meeting with Xi and Putin on Thursday, after the three met the prior evening during a welcome banquet for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen. It marked the first time they had met together in person.

In September 2025, Xi, Putin and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi briefly laughed together for a friendly photo-op alongside an annual meeting of the Shanghai Cooperation Organization in Tianjin, China. The gathering followed easing tensions between Beijing and New Delhi.

China's "ability to engage directly with the world major powers" reflects the country's influence across the Global South and Eurasia, said Jodie Wen, postdoctoral fellow at the Center for International Security and Strategy at Tsinghua University. "China hopes to play a more constructive role in this conflict-[ridden], unstable world."

In the future, "China will hope to provide more platforms" within its borders for countries to discuss issues, she said.

Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

China has increasingly played the role of broker, for instance, providing the forum for restoring diplomatic ties between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023.

By participating in and hosting different multilateral organizations, China has stayed in the Western orbit while cultivating relations with non-Western countries. The two photo-ops last year and this year reflect only a portion of Xi's large number of meetings with different world leaders.

Xi has hosted more than 40 heads of state so far this year, including at least eight around the APEC meetings in Shenzhen this week. That's on top of receiving over 60 state visits in 2025, according to CNBC analysis of official Chinese readouts.

Domestic Chinese media have highlighted how Xi has met with many foreign leaders around the China-hosted events.

"Beijing sees these multilateral platforms as opportunities to expand its international influence and demonstrate its commitment to multilateralism, while bilateral diplomacy remains equally important," said Yue Su, principal economist for China at EIU.

But she noted that different countries increasingly pursue different geopolitical and economic priorities, making it challenging to reach consensus even through multilateral organizations.

"Against this backdrop, China's diplomatic priorities are increasingly focused on delivering tangible economic benefits through trade, investment, financial connectivity and technology cooperation," she said.

On Thursday, Xi announced at the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting that China would provide $20 million in support for the Asia Pacific region. He also advocated for the development of an "Asia-Pacific community with a shared future," an iteration of his commonly used slogan on a "shared future" for humanity more broadly.

The Shenzhen summit can create meaningful outcomes for crisis management, as well as diplomatic space for future negotiations, Tsinghua University's Wen said. She added that it's important for leaders to come together in person in order to avoid misunderstandings and further tensions.

Trump said Thursday at the trilateral that the U.S., China and Russia are "uniquely positioned" to discuss reducing nuclear weapons at their historic meeting. A Chinese readout subsequently said the three leaders discussed Ukraine, the Middle East and the Korean Peninsula.

—CNBC's Matthew Tan contributed to this report.

#2883. Ohio State’s playoff hopes aren’t dead, but they need some help

There is something uncomfortably foreign about Ohio State fans spending the final two weeks of November trying to figure out how a three-loss Buckeye football team can sneak into the College Football Playoff. For a program with OSU's history, resources, talent, and expectations, this is not exactly where we envisioned ourselves when the season began. And yet, here we are, desperately searching for a way to turn a deeply disappointing season into one that still has the potential to end with a national championship.

Fortunately, the rest of the college football hierarchy has spent much of the 2026 season demonstrating that practically everybody else is also as flawed, leaving the door ever so slightly ajar for Ohio State to potentially slip into the playoff field. So, while OSU's postseason hopes are hanging by a thread, that thread is considerably stronger than it was just a week ago.

Following their victory over Northwestern, the Buckeyes jumped six spots to No. 14 in the latest College Football Playoff rankings. That's obviously not yet high enough to get into the field, but considering they were sitting at No. 20 following the loss to Oregon, it's a meaningful improvement.

Now, with just Nebraska and Michigan remaining on the regular-season schedule, the question is relatively straightforward: What has to happen for the Buckeyes to make the 12-team field? The answer, unfortunately, is considerably less straightforward.

First things first: Ohio State has to look like a playoff team

Before we start breaking down all of the cockamamie outcomes we need to start rooting for to give the Buckeyes a shot at the CFP, there is one rather significant piece of this puzzle that Ohio State actually controls: The Buckeyes have to win their final two regular-season games. And, frankly, I don't think simply surviving those games is going to be good enough.

Ohio State needs to travel to Lincoln and beat Nebraska convincingly before returning home and absolutely blowing the doors off of That Team Up North. I'm not suggesting the Buckeyes need to win both games by 30 points, but that certainly wouldn't hurt. At minimum, they need to demonstrate that the problems plaguing them over the past month are being addressed.

That starts with better offensive game planning and play calling, improved execution from the offensive line, and more consistent quarterback play from Luke Fahey. It also means making better use of Jeremiah Smith and the other offensive weapons, rather than continuing to operate an offense that occasionally seems determined to make moving the football as difficult as humanly possible.

Defensively, the Buckeyes need to rediscover the consistency that made them one of the country's most formidable units earlier this season.

And perhaps most importantly, they need to stop sabotaging themselves on both sides of the ball with penalties, turnovers, dropped passes, and the kind of unforced errors that have turned manageable situations into completely unnecessary adventures.

The CFP committee doesn't explicitly reward running up the score, but it does evaluate how teams play. If Ohio State finishes 9-3 by looking noticeably better against Nebraska and Michigan, it becomes considerably easier to argue that the Buckeyes are a dangerous postseason opponent rather than a fading team being propped up by its reputation.

And if the improvements aren't there, I'm not particularly interested in arguing that this team should be one of the 12 competing for a national championship.

The playoff math is a little more complicated than No. 14 suggests

Although Ohio State sits just two spots outside the top 12, the Buckeyes actually have a little more climbing to do than that.

Under this year's playoff format before it expands to approximately 139 teams next year, the champions of the ACC, Big Ten, Big 12, and SEC receive automatic bids, as does the highest-ranked team from the Group of Six conferences. Notre Dame also receives an automatic bid if it finishes inside the committee's top 12.

Currently, Boise State is the highest-ranked Group of Six team at No. 17, meaning the Broncos would take the final playoff spot despite being ranked behind several Power Four teams.

That leaves No. 11 Wake Forest as the last team in the projected field, while No. 12 Texas Tech, No. 13 Oregon, and No. 14 Ohio State are the first three teams out.

Essentially, the Buckeyes need to move into the top 11, and potentially even higher if the conference championship games produce additional bid thieves. That means passing at least three teams currently ahead of them, while also avoiding being leapfrogged by anyone behind them.

The good news is that there are plenty of opportunities for teams to stumble. The bad news is that Ohio State doesn't have another ranked opponent on its schedule to strengthen its résumé, so the Buckeyes are going to need other programs to do some of the heavy lifting.

Who should Ohio State fans be rooting against?

Here's where things get interesting, because the final two weekends feature several games with the potential to dramatically change the playoff picture.

No. 13 Oregon: This is probably the most obvious place to start, even though it is a bit complicated, since the Ducks beat OSU just a few weeks ago. However, since Oregon is just one spot ahead of Ohio State and owns that head-to-head victory, they would be particularly difficult to pass if both teams finish with three losses. The Ducks took care of business against Michigan State on Friday night, leaving next weekend's home game against Washington as the Buckeyes' last realistic opportunity to get some help from Oregon. A fourth loss for the Ducks would eliminate one of Ohio State's most significant obstacles.

No. 12 Texas Tech: The Red Raiders are 9-1, but currently sit outside the projected field. They visit Baylor today before hosting TCU on Thanksgiving, and a loss in either game would create an opportunity for Ohio State to narrow the gap. Even better would be a scenario in which Texas Tech loses again and fails to win the Big 12 championship, because an automatic bid would render its regular-season losses considerably less important.

No. 10 BYU: The Cougars have already suffered two losses, and they have potentially tricky games at Kansas this weekend and against Cincinnati next Saturday. Another loss would put them at three and make them a much more direct competitor with Ohio State. Buckeye fans should also be rooting for No. 2 Utah to take care of business and win the Big 12, rather than allowing a lower-ranked team to claim an automatic berth while Utah remains safely in the field. How do you all feel about rooting for UC next Saturday?

No. 11 Wake Forest: The Demon Deacons might be one of the most important teams to watch. At 9-1, they visit Georgia Tech on Saturday before closing the regular season against Duke. A loss in either game would help, but Ohio State would still need the committee to prefer the Buckeyes' three-loss résumé over Wake Forest's two-loss one. Another loss in the ACC Championship Game could create an even more interesting debate, provided Miami wins the conference and doesn't introduce another unexpected automatic qualifier into the equation.

No. 9 Tennessee: The Volunteers are 8-2 and have upcoming games against LSU and Vanderbilt. A loss to either would give Tennessee three losses and put the Vols into the same general conversation as Ohio State. Their victories over Alabama and Kentucky would still make them formidable competition in the selection room, but at least the comparison would become more realistic. However, considering the fact that the committee bows down to all things SEC, the Vols would likely need to lose both remaining games to fall below the Buckeyes, regardless of how good OSU looks down the stretch.

There are additional possibilities involving teams like Kentucky, Iowa, and USC, but those programs are currently ranked behind Ohio State. The Buckeyes certainly don't need any of them making a late surge, particularly USC, which also holds a head-to-head victory over OSU.

Ultimately, the ideal scenario would involve Oregon losing to Washington, Texas Tech dropping at least one of its remaining games, and at least one of BYU, Wake Forest, or Tennessee taking another loss. That wouldn't automatically guarantee the Buckeyes a playoff spot, but it would make the argument for their inclusion considerably more compelling.

There's also the Big Ten Championship Game possibility

Now, if you really want to dream, there is still a scenario in which Ohio State doesn't have to depend entirely on the committee's willingness to put a 9-3 team in the playoff. The Buckeyes can technically still reach the Big Ten Championship Game, although they need substantially more help to get there.

Assuming Ohio State beats Nebraska and Michigan, the Buckeyes would finish 7-2 in conference play. They would then need Indiana to lose at least one of its final two games against Washington and Purdue, while also getting help from Oregon, which could win a three-way tiebreaker involving the Buckeyes and Hoosiers. The cleanest general path would involve Ohio State winning out, Oregon losing to Washington, and Indiana dropping at least one of its remaining games, allowing the Buckeyes to capitalize on their head-to-head victories over Indiana and Iowa.

There are additional permutations involving UCLA, Iowa, and the conference's tiebreakers, so those results alone aren't a guaranteed ticket to Indianapolis. But the relevant point is that Ohio State hasn't been mathematically eliminated from the conference championship race, and as unlikely as it might be, the Buckeyes could still find themselves playing for a Big Ten title.

If Ohio State somehow reaches Indianapolis and wins the Big Ten championship, all of this hand-wringing becomes irrelevant. The Buckeyes would receive an automatic playoff berth, regardless of their overall record. Of course, that would require winning three consecutive games against opponents of varying degrees of competence, something this team hasn't exactly inspired confidence in its ability to do lately.

Still, there's something undeniably appealing about the possibility of Ohio State stumbling through the middle of the season, sneaking into the conference title game, and then winning the championship in the final year of the Big Ten's current title-game format. Stranger things have happened, although, admittedly, I'd appreciate it if the Buckeyes could start by making a few ordinary things happen consistently.

The biggest wildcard might be what happens on championship weekend

Even if Ohio State wins its final two games and gets the requisite help elsewhere, the Buckeyes could still find themselves sweating out conference championship weekend. The most frustrating possible outcome would be watching a lower-ranked team win a Power Four conference championship and steal an automatic berth while a highly ranked loser remains in the field. That's particularly relevant in the ACC and Big 12, where multiple teams could conceivably finish the season with résumés strong enough to warrant consideration.

Ideally, unless Ohio State ends up winning the B1G, it wants the strongest remaining contenders to win their conferences, keeping the number of automatic qualifiers who wouldn't otherwise earn at-large berths to a minimum.

In other words, once the regular season ends, Buckeye fans might find themselves rooting for favorites across the board and eschewing the chaos that we otherwise love about college football.

I still think Ohio State is far more likely to miss the College Football Playoff than make it, and, even if they do sneak in, I'm certainly not suggesting that a postseason berth would somehow erase all the problems that have plagued this team throughout the season. But the path is there, and it isn't quite as absurd as it looked following the back-to-back losses to USC and Oregon.

The Buckeyes have victories over No. 8 Indiana and No. 15 Iowa, and their one-point loss to fifth-ranked Texas looks better now than it did a month ago. Their losses to USC and Oregon are difficult to excuse, but the committee has already demonstrated that it isn't prepared to dismiss Ohio State entirely.

If the Buckeyes finish 9-3, beat Nebraska and Michigan convincingly, and benefit from a few strategically placed upsets, there is absolutely a credible argument for putting them in the field.

Would I necessarily agree that they deserve it? That's a different question, and one I've already spent plenty of time wrestling with. But whether Ohio State deserves a playoff spot and whether Ohio State can get a playoff spot are two entirely different conversations. And for the next two Saturdays, I'll be rooting very hard for both, because, after all, if college football is determined to embrace chaos, I see absolutely no reason why the Buckeyes shouldn't benefit from it for once.

#2882. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2881. Cowboys news: Jerry Jones says Dallas is a ‘bad team’

The Dallas Cowboys haven't been consistent when it comes to playing well as a team, and Jerry Jones made some very direct comments speaking to just that. Mike Pettine will takeover the defensive play calls from Christian Parker, but he insists that they're working side by side to get things turned around.

The safety position in Dallas has been ravaged by injuries with free agent addition Jalen Thompson missing the most time. He hasn't payed in nearly two months, but that could change in Week 11.

Team owner Jerry Jones was brutally honest when describing the Cowboys, and feels the recent move to take play-calling duties from defensive coordinator Christian Parker will benefit the team.

"We have not been a good team and are a bad team," Jones said Friday on 105.3 The Fan in Dallas, via Yahoo Sports. "At 4-6, that says everything you need to say. … Does that mean we will play a bad game [and] be a bad team on Sunday? Not necessarily. And that's why we're trying to improve."

One way the Cowboys tried to improve their ailing defense this week was to strip first-year defensive coordinator Christian Parker of his play-calling duties and give them to senior defensive assistant Mike Pettine.

Going forward, Mike Pettine will call the plays for the defense in Dallas. It was unexpected development, but he and Christian Parker are on the same page.

"[Parker] and I spoke about it, but it's really since then been business as usual," Pettine said. "I think you see his character and how he's handled this with poise and professionalism. I had a ton of respect for him before, I have even more now. This is not an easy situation, but he knows as I know having been in the NFL, this is a tough sport for tough people. But it's something, you know, the bottom line is production on Sunday. That's been our focus since the move was made, and we've been joined at the hip putting a plan together and getting these guys ready to go."

Safety Jalen Thompson hasn't played since the end of September. He has a chance to get back on the field this Sunday.

Cowboys safety Jalen Thompson was listed as questionable on the final practice report of the week. Thompson is still on injured reserve, though the Cowboys opened his 21-day practice window Wednesday. He's moved around well in the three practices since.

Thompson hasn't played since suffering a hamstring injury in Week 3 against the Baltimore Ravens in Rio de Janeiro. Cowboys head coach Brian Schottenheimer said recently that Thompson hadn't had a setback, in regard to a question about the lengthy recovery, and that the injury was a serious one.

#2880. NBA Cup 2026: James Harden puts up 51 in Cavs' win over Pacers; shorthanded Raptors fend off Pistons, end 6-game skid

James Harden played by far his best game in a Cleveland Cavaliers uniform on Friday night during NBA Cup group play.

The 37-year-old guard erupted for 51 points in a 128-119 home win over the Indiana Pacers, a season high and his most since joining the Cavaliers amid the 2025-26 campaign.

Cleveland (9-8) improved to 2-1 in Cup competition, knocking on the Miami Heat's door atop East Group B. Indiana (5-11), which trailed the Cavaliers by as many as 24 points, dropped to 1-2 in this year's NBA Cup.

Harden shot 15 of 26 from the floor, including 7 of 11 from beyond the arc. He also finished 14 of 14 from the charity stripe and with six rebounds and six assists. The one-time NBA MVP and 11-time All-Star now has the third-most 50-plus-point games in NBA history. With 26 such performances, he passed the late Kobe Bryant (25) on Friday and now trails Michael Jordan (31) and Wilt Chamberlain (118) in that category.

While collecting his first 50-piece with the Cavaliers, Harden moved into ninth all-time for most combined NBA regular and postseason points. His handles were on full display. He rained down 3s, and he made an impact at the rim.

Toronto came into Friday missing a trio of starters and on a six-game losing streak. Darko Rajaković's team was playing without RJ Barrett, Collin Murray-Boyles and, of course, Kawhi Leonard, who's still working his way back from a knee injury.

And yet, the shorthanded Raptors (6-10) found a way on the road versus the Detroit Pistons (10-5) in NBA Cup action, ending their skid with a riveting 105-102 win.

It's important to note that Detroit isn't at full strength, either. Jalen Duren remains sidelined with a hamstring issue. Plus, Duncan Robinson is out with a quad strain.

The absence of some star power didn't diminish what turned out to be an incredible matchup between two squads in East Group A and between a pair of former Montverde Academy teammates. Toronto's Scottie Barnes and Detroit's Cade Cunningham went toe-to-toe, particularly down the stretch.

Cunningham, who clocked out with 23 points and nine assists, hit a go-ahead 3-pointer over Barnes with less than 20 seconds remaining. Then, with the Raptors down one point, Barnes drove on Cunningham and reclaimed the lead.

Cunningham tried to answer back but ended up with nowhere to go with the ball after picking up his dribble en route to the basket. He passed it, and Toronto rookie Allen Graves nabbed a steal that helped wrap up the victory.

Barnes had 29 points, 5 rebounds and 5 assists. Graves, a first-round pick from Santa Clara making his first career start, piled up 20 points, 10 rebounds and 3 steals.

After that nail-biter, Toronto is 2-1 in East Group A, whereas Detroit is 1-1. The bottom dweller of the group, the Milwaukee Bucks (0-2 in NBA Cup play), was eliminated from knockout-round contention because the Raptors and Orlando Magic both won on Friday.

The Magic left Brooklyn victorious 113-111, thanks to Franz Wagner, who scored 35 points on 13-of-25 shooting and knocked down a game-winning jumper with 2.4 ticks left against the Nets.

Orlando is 3-0 in NBA Cup games this year.

#2878. One thing Patriots must fix coming out of Week 11 bye week

The biggest thing that the New England Patriots need to fix after their 35-10 loss to the Detroit Lions last week is their running game, which offered little support for the team's offense. Establishing consistent production on the ground must come first as New England evaluates its shortcomings.

Corey Kiner led the Patriots' backfield with 25 yards on 10 carries against Detroit. Starter Rhamondre Stevenson did play through injury, but still managed only four yards with the six carries he was given, leaving the pair with just 29 yards on 16 rushes. Neither back provided the sustained production needed to keep the offense balanced.

Kiner's performance marked a step backward from Week 9, when he gained 55 yards and scored a touchdown on eight carries against Green Bay. As for Stevenson, while his efforts while fighting through injury have been commendable, he struggled for a second consecutive week after rushing six times for 32 yards against the Packers, extending concerns about the Patriots' offensive backfield with TreVeyon Henderson out.

Admittedly, the Patriots' offensive line has dealt with a wave of injuries this season, with starting guards Mike Onwenu and Alijah Vera-Tucker both still on injured reserve, Ben Brown having missed the game against the Lions and Greg Van Roten having returned from injury last week. However, with Vera-Tucker being eligible to return from IR next week, reinforcements might be on the way, which could bode well for the team moving forward.

The Patriots need Henderson's explosiveness to jolt their offense and turn routine carries into meaningful gains. His big-play ability would provide a missing element to New England's offense once he returns from injury, but improved blocking and execution remain essential as well. New England needs dependable rushing production moving forward to string together more wins for the rest of the season.

The team might be able to fix its issues on the ground by signing a free agent to aid their efforts while Stevenson works his way back to 100 percent health and Henderson recovers. Or, they could give second-year running back Lan Larison more carries to see what he is capable of giving the team.

Given the fact that Patriots quarterback Drake Maye got hurt in the game and was replaced by Tommy DeVito, the run game became the biggest glaring issue that held the team back from having a more productive outing, and it is what needs to be addressed most prior to Week 12.

#2876. James Harden erupts for 51 to pass Kobe Bryant on 50-point games list

James Harden turned back the clock Friday, Nov. 20, reminding NBA opponents and fans why he is one of the best scorers in league history.

The 37-year-old scored 51 points Friday to lead the Cavaliers to a 128-119 win over the Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup East Group B game at Rocket Arena in Cleveland.

Harden's 51 points were the most he's scored in a game this year and came two days shy of the one-year anniversary of his last 50-point game, when, as a member of the Los Angeles Clippers, he shelled out 55 in a win against the Charlotte Hornets on Nov. 22, 2025.

But his scoring output Friday night represented a more meaningful milestone: Harden notched his 26th 50-point game of his career, passing Kobe Bryant for third all time in NBA history. Michael Jordan ranks second with 31 such games, while the all-time leader, Wilt Chamberlain, seems likely to retain that record for a very long time having crossed the 50-point threshold an astounding 118 times.

Only three other players have scored at least 50 points this season, and all three are MVP candidates. Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić has passed the 50-point mark twice, tallying 53 against the Portland Trail Blazers on Nov. 5 and 52 against the Utah Jazz on Oct. 25. San Antonio Spurs star Victor Wembanyama dropped 52 against the Dallas Mavericks on Nov. 4, while Detroit Pistons guard Cade Cunningham scored 52 in a double overtime win against the Washington Wizards on Nov. 12.

Harden ranks ninth in NBA history and 10th in NBA-ABA history for most career points with 29,633 in 1,237 games played. Next on the career list is Hall of Famer Julius Erving with 30,026 career points in the NBA and ABA. Philadelphia 76ers star LeBron James holds the record for most points in league history with 43,628 (through Nov. 20).

#2874. Sean Payton gives curious answer on Broncos’ vision for Jahdae Barron

With the recent extension of cornerback Riley Moss and the desire for the Denver Broncos to revisit contract negotiations with Ja'Quan McMillian, many fans across Broncos Country have wondered what the vision is for second-year cornerback Jahdae Barron.

The Broncos have locked up both their boundary corners long-term in Moss and Patrick Surtain II. Knowing that, it's a given that Barron isn't going to see any significant reps on the outside in the near future unless an injury were to occur. If McMillian departs in free agency after this season, kicking him inside to the nickel spot to replace Ja'Quan makes a lot of sense. Though that's a bridge the team will have to wait and cross in due time.

Yesterday after practice, Head Coach Sean Payton was asked how Barron fit in with the defense. I thought Payton gave a curious answer. Your mileage may vary, but this is what he had to say on the issue.

"It's all year-to-year. In other words, relative to Jahdae, he's got that flexibility to play in the dime stuff, play outside," stated Payton.

With Denver's cornerbacks now healthy, it seems likely, based off the aforementioned, that Barron's only chance of seeing the field this year [barring injury] is going to be in Defensive Coordinator Vance Joseph's dime packages. That's not necessarily the news Broncos Country wants to hear, with many of them longing for the 2025 first-round pick to have a larger role in the defense.

Moving forward, as noted, things do remain fluid. On multiple occasions, Payton and Joseph have touted Barron's outside and inside flexibility. One thought that has rolled through my mind, as well as many of yours, is whether or not Jahdae could have a future at the safety position—potentially replacing Brandon Jones, who is a free agent at the end of this season.

Back when Payton was coaching the New Orleans Saints, they drafted Ohio State standout Malcolm Jenkins in the first round of the 2009 NFL Draft. Like Barron, Jenkins was also a Jim Thorpe Award winner. During his rookie season, he spent time on the boundary but had most of his snaps inside at the nickel in Gregg Williams' defense.

The following year, he would move to free safety, a position he would man for the Saints and Philadelphia Eagles for the next twelve years of his career. Asked as a follow up to the first question, Payton gave his answer on Barron's ability to play safety. He kept it short and sweet.

"That's not something we've thought about or discussed."

All things considered, I am a bit surprised by Payton's answer. Though the fluidity with the year-to-year comment does make a bit of sense. Given Barron's versatility and ability to play multiple roles, he's a good player to have in the defense. But the commentary given by Payton makes it a little ambiguous as to what the future has in store for Barron.

For now, apparently Barron will be relegated to dime package looks. Beyond this season? That'll be determined with whatever moves they make in the offseason.

What do you think, Broncos Country? Give me your thoughts on Payton's comments and where you see Barron fitting in with the team moving forward.

#2873. Warren Buffett quadruples Thanksgiving gifts: A breakdown of how much wealth he's giving away

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Warren Buffett, 96, more than quadrupled his Thanksgiving donations to four family foundations this year to $5.5 billion.

The gifts consist of Berkshire Class B shares that have been converted from his roughly $160 billion of Class A shares.

The increase is in line with his new goal, announced last July, to give away all of his Berkshire Hathaway shares "within about eight years," with no additional annual donations going to the Gates Foundation.

Around $2.2 billion of shares goes to the Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, named for his late first wife, with the three foundations run by his children, Susie Buffett's Sherwood Foundation, the Howard G. Buffett Foundation, and Peter Buffett's NoVo Foundation each getting $1.1 billion each.

In July of last year, Buffett gave shares worth $4.6 billion to the Gates Foundation with another $1.4 billion going to the family foundations.

This July, the family foundations received $5.9 billion in stock, bringing the year's total to $11.4 billion, an increase of almost 55% from last year, even as the Gates Foundation received nothing.

In comments included in the Berkshire news release this week announcing the new gifts, Buffett reaffirmed his confidence in his children's ability to disburse his fortune over time, saying they "have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity."

He also said his confidence in Berkshire is "as strong as ever."

Recalling that in last year's lengthier Thanksgiving message he had said, "Kindness is costless but also priceless," Buffett wrote, " A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie [Munger] and I ever bought."

"Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received.

"Much of my good fortune has come in the form of people who were kind to me and expected nothing in return. I'm grateful to every one of them.

"This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

Berkshire Hathaway's fourth quarter rally that had produced a string of record highs cooled this week as both classes of its stock posted five straight daily declines.

Last Friday, Berkshire was up 19.1% since October 1. Now that gain has been cut to 14.5%

The S&P's decline of 8.6% this week, however, was even bigger than Berkshire's 5-day drop, as investors sold to lock in profits from AI-related stocks.

The benchmark index, along with the Dow Industrials, are in a correction after falling 10% from their recent highs, and the Nasdaq Composite has erased all of its year-to-date gains.

Berkshire's smaller decline increased its year-to-date outperformance of the S&P to 12.5 percentage points.

One Wall Street firm, however, thinks Berkshire's run-up has run its course.

Argus Research downgraded the B shares to hold from buy Friday, saying the stock is "fully valued" at about 25 times next year's estimate for operating earnings per share.

As foreshadowed by its 10-Q financial report released almost two weeks ago, Berkshire Hathaway's Q3 13F portfolio snapshot filed with the SEC on Monday showed it wasn't doing much buying and selling of equities this summer.

And the two largest additions, Lennar and Bending Spoons, had been previously disclosed, along with additional purchases at the start of the fourth quarter.

The most notable change we didn't expect was a 12% increase in Berkshire's Delta Air Lines position, bringing it just short of a 10% stake, now valued at $5.2 billion.

Exceeding 10% triggers a number of regulatory burdens, including the requirement to promptly disclose new buying or selling, so we may not see additional purchases going forward.

Delta's return to the portfolio after Warren Buffett sold all of Berkshire's airline stocks in 2020 has been something of a surprise given his bumpy ride with the industry over the years.

As expected, Berkshire continued to trim its Bank of America stake. After nine consecutive quarters of sales, it's been cut by 56% but remains the fifth largest portfolio holding at $21.4 billion.

Berkshire also cut another financial, Capital One, by almost 62%, although it was already a relatively small position, now worth $207 million.

Also notable are two positions that didn't change.

After adding $17 billion in Q2 to the Alphabet bet that had only been initiated last summer, Berkshire defied some expectations by keeping the holding unchanged.

Its 106 million shares are valued at $28.1 billion.

And for the fourth consecutive quarter, Berkshire's largest equity holding, Apple, didn't change. With a big boost from the stock's year-to-date gain of 26.1%, the position is now valued at more than $78 billion.

Warren Buffett loves to eat what many would call junk food and drink a lot of sugary beverages. A quarter-century ago, when he was 70, Buffett explained why he still thought a life insurance company would rate his life expectancy "considerably better than the average."

AUDIENCE MEMBER: Every time I hear what you like to eat, Warren, it makes me wonder what your cholesterol level is — (laughter) — or if you even worry about it...

WARREN BUFFETT: I do know the number, and I don't remember it. My doctor tells me, "It's a little high," but if he says it's a little high, it means it isn't that high, or he would — because he always tries to push me into making a few changes in my life.

But he — I've got a wonderful doctor. And I was lucky last year, because I hadn't been in to see him for about five years. And — (laughter) — due to — those guys cost a lot of money, I mean. (Laughter)

And due to purely an accident, a reaction to some other medicine I was given when I was out of the city, he got a hold of me, and then he shamed me into having a physical. And it was extremely lucky, because I had a polyp in the colon that would have probably caused trouble, you know, within a couple of years.

I would say that if you ask my doctor, he would want me to make a few changes, but he would also say that my life expectancy is probably a lot better than the average person of 70.

You know, I have no stress whatsoever. Zero. You know, I mean, I get to do what I love to do every day. You know, and I'm surrounded by people that are terrific.

So that problem in life just doesn't exist for me. You know, and I don't smoke or drink or, well, we'll end it right there. (Laughter)

And so, you know, if you were an underwriter for a life company, you would rate me considerably better than the average. You'd rate Charlie better than average, too.

And I'm sure that, you know, I could change it slightly, perhaps, on the probabilities, you know, if I change my diet dramatically or something. But it's very unlikely to happen.

#2871. Philadelphia mayor wants the Eagles to play in a dome

The Eagles will become an indoor team if the mayor of Philadelphia gets her way.

"I want a dome," Mayor Charelle L. Parker said on 93.3 WMMR, via the Philadelphia Inquirer. "Don't you tell. This is a secret between us. I just saw a rendering of this beautiful dome that they're talking about for the Bears."

Moving into a dome would change the character of late-season and playoff games in Philadelphia, which are often affected by snow, wind and cold. Plenty of fans won't like it. But politicians and NFL owners often prefer stadiums with roofs because they bring in lucrative events, indoor sports, concerts, conventions and trade shows.

The Eagles told season ticket holders last year that they are looking at either renovations to Lincoln Financial Field or a new stadium. Eagles owner Jeffrey Lurie has committed only to staying in "the region," not in the city of Philadelphia, but both Parker and Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro have said they want the Eagles to remain in the city.

The Eagles' lease at Lincoln Financial Field runs through the 2032 season.

#2870. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

#2869. Brooklyn Nets respond with a fight, but not a win, in 113-111 heartbreaker vs Magic

There is a funny irony to sports, especially the NBA.

You can spend hours and days dissecting what's wrong with a team, questioning everyone from the top of the organization down. The roster, the direction, and everything in between. And then, two nights later, the team goes out and completely contradicts the narrative, for better or worse.

After getting embarrassed by 30 points against the Chicago Bulls on Wednesday, the Nets entered Friday night's matchup against the Orlando Magic with expectations as low as they've been this season. The squad entered with a 3-11 record, with five of their losses coming by 15+ points, the most by any team in the league this season.

Yet, somehow, 48 hours later, the Nets looked like an entirely different basketball team.

The energy was palpable, the defensive effort was there, players were diving for loose balls, and the team looked noticeably better.

Danny Wolf, getting the start over Egor Dëmin, delivered 20 points and four 3-pointers. Julius Randle added 27 points, nine rebounds, and six assists while Mikel Brown Jr. contributed 18 points off the bench, tied for his career-high.

And for a while, it looked like the Nets might actually have something to celebrate. Instead, they were reminded of another cruel reality of basketball: you could do a lot right and still lose games.

Despite overcoming an early 13-point deficit and taking a fourth-quarter lead, Brooklyn fell to Orlando, 113-111, on a Franz Wagner game-winner with under three seconds to play. Wagner finished with 35 points. It could have been a signature win for the team, but instead, it ended in heartbreaking fashion.

With 2.4 seconds left, the Nets got the ball back and had a chance to seal it, but Julius Randle missed an open game-winning 3-pointer.

"Julius had the ball in his hands… that's what I wanted," said Jordi Fernandez post-game. "He shot a step back and it was wide open, and I live with it,"

The game shouldn't have even come down to that moment, though. Unfortunately for the Brooklyn Nets, there isn't a standing for moral victories, and they now fall to 3-12.

The Nets jumped out to a 17-11 lead in the first quarter before Orlando started to catch up. By the end of the first frame, the score had flipped, with the Magic up 38-25.

Brooklyn's biggest early problem was stopping the Magic from shooting from deep. Without strong perimeter defense, which the Nets have lacked all season, it's going to stay tough for them to beat teams with more roster talent, like Orlando.

To the team's credit, though, there was a clear increase in effort and pride, which had been in question over the past few games. Perhaps the "internal discussions," as Michael Porter Jr. described them, after Wednesday's blowout sent a message.

From Julius Randle to Mikel Brown Jr., everyone looked to be giving their all early on, diving for loose balls, and everything that comes with being a high-motor team even if they were trailing.

Subsequently, the Nets started to catch up. With under eight minutes to play in the second quarter, Brooklyn cut the score to 42-38 after a strong run fueled by Michael Porter Jr. MPJ, who scored 14 points in his first 13 minutes of the night after finishing with just five points in 27 minutes against the Bulls on Wednesday night.

Fernández also made a change to his starting lineup on Friday night. Danny Wolf replaced Egor Dëmin, who had been in a rough patch entering the game. It also marked his first time coming off the bench since November of last season. That decision worked out, as Wolf thrived while Dëmin's struggles continued, scoring six points in 21 minutes on 1-of-6 shooting, including a 0-of-3 mark from 3-point range.

"We were struggling with rebounding and with our defense, and I felt like Danny was playing well," Fernandez said, adding. "Obviously, there's no guarantees or anything for anybody."

Entering halftime, the Nets trailed just 62-61, chipping away at the 13-point deficit they faced at the end of the first frame. It was a battle from beyond the arc, as Orlando continued to find success from deep, hitting a whopping 57.1% of their tries, while Brooklyn converted 41.2% of their 3-point attempts.

The Nets also did a strong job of forcing the Magic into mistakes, forcing 12 turnovers in the first half compared to their eight. The team also capitalized on the extra possessions, scoring 18 points off turnovers.

Coming out of halftime, the Nets stayed in the game but didn't do enough to take the lead entering the fourth quarter, still trailing 91-88. Nolan Traoré left the game with a hamstring injury during the third frame and was quickly ruled out, limping into the locker room.

Asked about whether Traore injury was serious, Fernandez responded: "I don't know."

Traoré has been a crucial piece in the Nets' backcourt this season, but when he went down, rookie Mikel Brown Jr. stepped up in a big way.

Brown logged 13 points in the third quarter, keeping the Nets in the game while hitting both of his shots from beyond the arc. By the end of the frame, he had logged 15 points in 18 minutes. Fernandéz sent him to the bench to begin the fourth quarter, giving Keon Ellis his first minutes of the night, before returning with just over six minutes remaining in the final frame.

Early on in the fourth quarter, the Nets grabbed their first lead since the first half. Wolf had a breakaway dunk and a crucial 3-pointer, marking his fourth deep make in moments, while Julius Randle hit his first three of the game. It brought the score to 98-93 with 8:35 left, forcing Orlando to use a timeout.

But as the Nets have learned plenty of times this season, building a lead and protecting one are two very different things. Orlando worked their way back, setting up a dramatic finish. And despite the Nets' improved play throughout the night, it was the Magic who closed out the game.

With the game tied 111 in the closing seconds, Wagner knocked down a contested fadeaway jumper from just inside the 3-point line with 2.4 seconds remaining, putting Orlando ahead by two. Brooklyn still had a chance to steal the game, but Randle's potential game-winning 3-pointer missed.

Just like that, the Nets had another loss to their record, falling 113-111.

Randle still led Brooklyn with 27 points, nine rebounds, six assists, and three steals, shooting 9-of-19 from the field. Day'Ron Sharpe also continued his strong start to the year, adding another double-double with 17 points and 11 rebounds.

Meanwhile, Wolf arguably had his best game as a pro, finishing with 20 points on 6-of-10 shooting overall, including 4-of-6 from deep. Post-game, he talked about his game.

"It's hard to come up here and keep having these moral victories. There's been a lot of those this year. I think we responded very well after that Chicago game. But with how hard guys played, we really wanted that win and we needed that win."

Meanwhile, Clowney's struggles continued, as he went scoreless on four field-goal attempts in 22 minutes and the Wagner game-winner came over him on defense.

The positive spin is that the Nets showed much more fight than they did two nights ago, but there's still no win to show for it.

Although the Nets called up three playes from the G League, Ben Saraf, Drake Powell and Joshua Jefferson didn't play, with only Saraf suiting up.

Final Score: Orlando Magic, 113, Brooklyn Nets, 111

Friday also marked the first matchup between the Wagner brothers: Franz and Mo. Safe to say Franz took home the family bragging rights.

With their parents looking on, Franz and Moe Wagner shared some salty but mostly emotional talk. The 6'10" 25-year-old Franz is four years younger than the 6'11" 29-year-old Moe.

"It's awkward, I'm not gonna lie. I don't really know how to compartmentalize it, really deal with it, and try to focus on the game plan and see what happens emotionally," Moe said pre-game. "It's a dilemma because you want him to do well. At the same time, you game plan for him, you prepare to limit him. So, it's an awkward feeling. I've never been in this position."

"My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him," Franz said. "But as weird as it's going to be, it's a blessing to share the court with your family.Not a lot of people get to do that, so that's how I look at it."

In the end, of course, it was Franz's game, scoring 35 points, bringing his season averages to 24.4 points, 8.5 assists and 6.9 rebounds per game while Moe, who went into the game as the NBA's leading 3-point shooter, put up three points.

"He's playing incredible," Moe said. "New coach, new system, found a way to be a playmaker and scorer at the same time, dominating the pace, the paint. Obviously with an enormous usage rate as well, which you've got to give him credit for the physicality he plays with … He's a joy to watch."

A lot of Nets fans would like to see the Wagner brother reunited in Brooklyn but that seems unlikely. Franz has become the face of the franchise in Orlando and is under contract through 2029-30 and is owed $185 million.

The Nets will have two days off before heading to Minnesota, where they will face the 11-5 Timberwolves on Monday. Tip-off is set for 7:30 p.m. ET.

It will be another uphill battle for the 3-12 Nets, who continue to spiral.

Monday will mark Julius Randle's first matchup against Minnesota since Brooklyn acquired him over the summer. It will also be Brooklyn's first of two nationally televised games this year.

#2868. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.
