#2841. Game Recap: Suns survive late Jazz rally to secure 111-101 NBA Cup victory

Man, this game was an emotional rollercoaster, especially late, but the Suns pulled this one out in a big way. For a team that started 1-5, they have turned things around quickly, winning three straight and only losing two in their last nine games. This is a good trend for a team still figuring things out on the fly with their starting lineup. It's another big win, as this was an NBA Emirates Cup game, which helped them bounce back after their first loss in Group A to the Denver Nuggets.

This game started hot for Phoenix, who took a commanding lead in the first half and held on to it through the third as a lot of free throws were made. In the fourth, the Jazz tried to climb back, cutting the deficit to four, but it was too little, too late as the Suns took care of business on the road. Even though the Suns allowed 17 OREB, their 19 points on 15 turnovers helped them early and led to this deserved win.

Devin Booker led Phoenix with 23 points, two rebounds, and three assists, while Miles Bridges and Dillon Brooks each had 18. Bench mob beasts Collin Gillespie and Jordan Goodwin sealed the win: Gillespie added 16 points, 4 rebounds, and 5 assists, while Goodwin grabbed 9 rebounds and 4 assists, doing much of the dirty work alongside Bridges.

For the Jazz, Darryn Peterson had 20 points, ten rebounds, and eight assists, which was close to a triple-double, while Jaxson Hayes caused havoc on the glass. He finished with 18 points and 14 rebounds, a big reason Utah stayed in this one. Brice Sensabaugh also got hot late, adding 18 of his own points with five threes to keep the Jazz thriving from behind the arc.

For the Phoenix Suns, coming out of the gate hot was needed to get to a three-game win streak. For the Suns, though, before they could score a basket, they had to win their first challenge call that night because of the refs. The Suns starters did look solid in their first stint, as they got Dillon Brooks going early, with Keyonte George guarding him for an easy four points. Phoenix got off to an early run, but the Jazz matched it and tied it up at 12.

Sadly for the Suns, they would have to use their second challenge just seven minutes into the game when Booker was hit across the face by a driving Ace Bailey. Even though Phoenix won both challenges, it was tough to lose them with 40 minutes of game time left. This allowed the bench to come in and resume where they left off. Jordan Goodwin and Collin Gillespie made it known when they got out there, with Gillespie making the first three of the night for both teams.

With Khaman Maluach and Ryan Dunn checking in, the Suns seemed more aggressive on the boards and limited the Jazz's second-chance points. The Suns gave up 12 OREB in the first half, and that cannot continue into the second half. With the Jazz finding some threes late, they brought the game closer to the end of the first quarter, but Booker made the last shot count, hitting a buzzer-beater off a full-court pass from Miles Bridges. The Suns led 26-23 at the end of the first quarter.

Heading into the second, we saw the bench continue to prove they are the best in the league, with Gillespie making some tough buckets and Luke Kennard getting an easy five points on two quick shots. Unfortunately, the trouble on the glass is an issue, as Jaxson Hayes is dominating and getting multiple tip-ins and one-and-one opportunities.

Though this was the case, Brooks and Bridges provided the Suns with the scoring to take their largest lead so far, 43-30, with a strong run and Bridges getting comfortable. Sadly, the Suns saw an injury when Ryan Dunn caught a knee to the thigh and went to the locker room. He provided solid defense and rebounding, allowing the offense to flow better with ball movement.

The Jazz, though, didn't want to go out quiet as Darryn Peterson started showing off his skills on both ends. Bailey secured a tip-in at the end, but the Suns saw some nice moves from Malauch in his second stint. The big caught a nice block and made some easy passes for putbacks and points. The Suns finished the first half up 61-49. Brooks and Gillepsie lead the way for the Suns with eleven points each, while Bridges. Booker and Maluach have eight. It's truly been a team game offensively, with the ball being shared, especially in the second unit. For the Jazz, Peterson has 12 points, while Hayes has 11, and Sensabaugh has 3 made threes. To win this game, the Suns have to win the rebounding battle and control the possession.

To kick off the second half, the Suns found out that Ryan Dunn would not return, leaving his minutes in limbo. That is where Koa Peat got some burn and, unfortunately, didn't get too much of it, as he picked up some early fouls. The Suns did a better job on the glass, limiting the Jazz's second-chance points, but the Jazz were still getting to the charity stripe.

This is where the game took a toll on me, as it dragged on with both teams finding opportunities to score only at the free-throw line. Brooks and Bridges, though, continued to show their offensive flow, with Bridges driving and delivering some nice paint scoring alongside some boards. The Suns got the lead back to 12, and Booker found a rhythm of his own, scoring 10 points in the quarter to bring his total to 18.

The thirteen Jazz turnovers really cost them in this game, and with the Suns doing a better job of limiting them on the glass, the Suns looked on track for an easy win if they closed out the fourth. Although this is the Phoenix Suns we are talking about, they always make your heart skip a beat, even when they play the worst teams.

The fourth quarter opened with Brice Sensabaugh getting red hot and quickly scoring four points. The Suns answered with tough shots of their own from Gillespie, but the Jazz weren't bowing out of this one. With more rim pressure from Hayes, the Jazz got back on the glass and caused some disruptions. Peterson also showed off his quickness to get to the cup and now had a double-double with some time left.

Bridges once again helped this team, alongside Booker, keep them afloat with some nice baskets. Brooks picked up his fifth, then eventually his sixth foul, and was fouled out of the game, which was not a good look as the Jazz rallied back. With more made threes, the Suns, who had a 16-point lead at one point, were down to just four with under a minute left.

Luckily for this team, Bridges was on tonight and made multiple nice inbound passes to Booker to get a guaranteed bucket, and here was another example of that. Booker then went on to get another three points by hunting his shots, and the Suns pulled off a 111-101 win. This is now the team's longest win streak this season, at three, and they just secured their first NBA Cup win this year. Overall, it wasn't the best win on paper, but it was solid nonetheless.

The Suns continue their road trip, heading to New Orleans to take on the Pelicans.

#2839. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2838. Big Blue View mailbag: Play calling, Malik Nabers, Dominic Zvada, more

Ben Fitzsimmons asks: I know Matt Nagy is the OC and is the one calling plays. I don't fully understand how Greg Roman fits into it. We've seen the Giants run it between the tackles at least 400 million times on first down this year. We've seen that terrible play from the infamous JJ Package show up a few times (still hasn't worked once btw). We've seen the Giants attempt and fail miserably to run the clock out at the end of games multiple times this year. We've seen a lack of creativity in the run game as they mostly just run behind Ricard and hope it works (it usually doesn't).

What I'm curious about is how much of that is Greg Roman and how much is Matt Nagy. Is Roman designing the run plays and Matt Nagy is just calling them? Is Roman just providing advice and vibes while Nagy draws up the plays? Does Roman have any impact on how run plays are called each game (I would assume not, but I've been wrong many times before)?

Ed says: Ben, Greg Roman's title is 'Senior Offensive Assistant'. Nagy runs the offense and makes all play-calling decisions. He makes final determination, with John Harbaugh's input, on the game plan. Roman is acknowledged as one of the best run-game designers in the game today. To my knowledge, Roman is responsible for designing much of the Giants' run game.

Harbaugh has explained that much of the vertical passing game comes from Nagy's background. As I've said, Roman's specialty is the run game.

Nagy's job, as he has explained it, is to take the menu of options he has in front of him and decide what to use and when to use it. Roman, who has been an offensive coordinator multiple times, sits in the booth with Nagy on game days. Of course there will be some sharing of information and ideas. The decisions, though, are Nagy's to make.

We see the warts. Sometimes, you make the wrong call. Sometimes, you make the right one and players don't execute it. The Giants are 14th in the league in score at 22.6 points per game. That's pretty good, considering that they are on their third quarterback and have struggled mightily to block in the run game.

Bob Donnelly asks: Following the Commanders' game you have questioned some of the offensive play calls, especially not running the ball in a few key situations. The OC makes the play call yet much of the blame/criticism seems to be directed towards the HC and his lack of confidence in his team's ability to run the ball in those situations.

In your view how much play calling influence does our head coach exert in critical situations?

Ed says: Bob, the reality is that the head coach gets credit when things go right, and everything that goes wrong also lands at his feet.

Harbaugh does not call the plays. He can set the direction, though. Does he want the ball run? Thrown? What personnel does he want in the game.

Tom Donlan asks: Malik Nabers can make spectacular catches and carry the passing game with a high volume of targets and catches. We've also seen drops and a costly fumble against Washington, as well as what some view as immature or disruptive behavior with poor body language on the field. What do you think he needs to do to become a more consistent No. 1 receiver, and do you know how the Giants view the way he voices his frustrations publicly?

Ed says: Tom, Malik Nabers has a drop rate this season of 3.5%, per Pro Football Reference. That isn't exactly terrible. He has fumbled three times in 185 career touches, one basically every 62 touches. Again, that isn't awful. Drops happen occasionally. No one who has the ball a lot goes through a career without fumbling at times.

I'm not getting into the body language debate. As far as becoming a more consistent No. 1 receiver, how about getting some consistent reps with the quarterback. Nabers barely had any time with Jaxson Dart last year. Nabers' reps were limited all summer as he came back from his knee injury, then Dart went down for the season in Week 2. Now, Nabers and J.J. McCarthy have worked together for a month.

My educated guess is the Giants would like him to choose his words more carefully. But, he was encouraged to speak his mind freely as a rookie, and he is still a 23-year-old kid learning to grow up with a much different coach than the one who drafted him.

One other thing I will say is that I still believe Nabers has to learn to do a better job fighting to protect his quarteerback. I'm thinking about that interception at the end of the Washington game. The receiver simply has to put up a fight there to make sure the ball gets knocked down and not intercepted.

Joel Story asks: I read an article saying that after the Giants/Eagles game on Nov. 8th, Kayvon Thibodeaux was fined $11,941 for taunting, Jeremiah Trotter was fined $6,428 for tripping, Zack Baun was fined $6,750 for illegal use of the helmet, and Andrew Mukuba was fined $9,041 for a hit on a defenseless player. Are these amounts correct? If so, do you know why the NFL/NFLPA have chosen to fine players more for taunting than for the other three actions which could potentially end other players' seasons or even their careers?

Ed says: Joel, yes those amounts are correct. The fine schedule, which you can see it for yourself, is collectively bargained.

Taj Siddiqi asks: JH is being criticized for keeping Dominic Zvada and let Jason Sanders walk. Sanders is performing like a pro while Zvada is struggling. Some people think one of the causes for his firing at Baltimore was his decision to stay with the kicker, whose name I can't recall. Is JH over confident regarding his ability to evaluate kickers? Is the guy at the Baltimore still there or is he even in the league?

Ed says: Taj, John Harbaugh has been around long enough to fully understand the ups and downs, and the risks, of choosing to ride with a rookie placekicker.

Harbaugh had a rookie placekicker in Baltimore last season. Tyler Loop was very good for the Ravens in 2025. He made 30 of 34 field goals (88.2%). Harbaugh got fired because the Ravens missed the playoffs and the organization felt like his tenure had run its course after 18 years. It just so happens that Loop missed a 44-yard field goal on the final play of that game that would have gotten Baltimore into the playoffs. It was his only miss of the year between 40 and 49 yards.

How does anyone criticize a coach for riding with a kicker who went 30 of 34? Loop is still with the Ravens, but he is injured and on IR.

Overconfident in his ability to evaluate kickers? Well, he and the Ravens drafted Jordan Stout in the fourth round in 2022. Stout is probably the best punter in the league. Sam Koch punted in Baltimore for 16 years, 14 of those for Harbaugh.

Justin Tucker was the best field goal kicker in the NFL for more than a decade, and was bombing 60+ yard kicks long before there were a bunch of other kickers in the league capable of doing it. I would say Harbaugh knows good kickers when he sees them. If Harbaugh ends up being wrong about Zvada, that will be the first kicker or punter he has been wrong about in his time as a head coach.

As for Jason Sanders, I saw a number of those practices in the spring. A day or two before he got cut I watched him try to kick a 50-ish yard field goal on a windy day, and I watched the ball basically die and go straight down a couple yards short. I didn't think he could handle the MetLife Stadium conditions, either.

Francis Castros asks: Can you provide the rehabilitation status of the following players and are they required to rehab at the Quest Diagnostics Training Center?

– Tyrone Tracy Jr. — RB (Hamstring)– Tremaine Edmunds — LB / MLB (Chest / Shoulder)– Braxton Berrios — WR (Foot – surgery)– Chauncey Golston — DE / Edge (Neck)– Jaxson Dart — QB (Knee – Meniscus)– Brian Burns — LB (Knee – ACL)– Roy Robertson-Harris — DE (Achilles)– Calvin Austin III — WR (Knee)– Rico Payton — CB (Undisclosed)– Jarrick Bernard-Converse — CB (Knee)– Gunner Olszewski — WR (Achilles)– Thaddeus Dixon — CB (Achilles)

Ed says: Francis, I really don't have much. The team is not required to, and does not, share information about players on IR.

I can tell you Tyrone Tracy should be back at some point in the next few weeks. I saw Brian Burns in the locker room after the Washington game. He is still on crutches. Jaxson Dart has been seen at the facility a couple of times by media, though I have not seen him myself. I have seen Roy Robertson-Harris around a couple of times.

Players are not required to rehab at the team facility. Many do, though, spend at least some of their time doing that.

Wesley Protheroe asks: I have a question regarding the utilization of the Giants' tight ends:

While Isaiah Likely has been outstanding, the Giants' "conventional" tight ends have been largely ineffective. Johnson is an unreliable pass catcher and only a fair blocker. Manhertz is a better blocker but has only one target all season and defenses don't have to account for him. Fidone has been mostly a healthy scratch but has shown good hands in the pre-season and caught his one target for 29 yards. Shouldn't the Giants consider replacing Manhertz with an interior lineman or at least throw to him periodically to keep defenses honest? Additionally, why not give Fidone a chance to play over Johnson. He is definitely the more natural receiver. Your thoughts?

Ed says: Wesley, the Giants are not going to intentionally throw to Chris Manhertz when they can throw to Malik Nabers, Isaiah Likely, Malachi Fields, or Darnell Mooney instead. Still, if he runs out in a pass pattern he does have to be covered.

I'm not replacing Manhertz with an offensive lineman who isn't used to being out on the end of the line of scrimmage. Honestly, though, while I can't point to specific plays it does feel to me like neither Manhertz nor Patrick Ricard are having the impact on the run game the Giants were hoping for.

In reference to Johnson vs. Fidone, I am curious if Fidone could play a bigger role. It is pretty clear, though, that the coaching staff prefers Johnson. Maybe part of the reason is that Johnson has also become a fairly significant part of the special teams.

David Silver asks: A lot of articles this week were taking a look at Abdul Carter, trying to make sense of his game. By any measure but sacks, he has been a destructive, disruptive force who makes quarterbacks uncomfortable, including a third-best speed to the QB of 2.97 seconds. It seems when he's not getting us a holding call, the QB is unloading the ball when they see him destroying another lineman. My question is, how often do the rush Carter from the blindside? I mean, if he is one of the fastest rushers to the QB in the league, what happens when you can't see him coming? More sacks, right?

Ed says: David, I don't have access to data on exactly where each of Carter's pass rushes come from. I'm sure it exists somewhere, but not in anything I have available.

What I can tell you is that Carter has played 503 defensive snaps, and has been used in a multitude of positions. He has been used 167 times at right outside linebacker, and 157 at left outside linebacker. That leaves 179 snaps where he has been aligned in a variety of other places, from nose tackle to middle linebacker.

From that, you have to extrapolate that defensive coordinator Dennard Wilson is already doing a good job moving Carter around.

Bruce Frazer asks: Donte Whitner on ESPN described JJ McCarthy as a one read quarterback, not ready to elevate the giants to a higher level. He showed a number of plays highlighting the problem showing JJ constantly keying on his first read and throwing to that receiver. Do you feel this is an issue that the coaches are aware of this.

Ed says: Bruce, c'mon now! This has been McCarthy's reputation since his Michigan days. It's hardly a revelation. He threw far less in college than the other quarterbacks in that draft class. He's played in 15 NFL games — less than a full season. This is a common issue with young, inexperienced quarterbacks. Of course the coaching staff is aware of it.

Have a Giants-related question? E-mail it to bigblueview@gmail.com and it might be featured in our weekly mailbag.

#2837. Grand Prix Finland: Mao Shimada wins, Isabeau Levito climbs to podium

Mao Shimada extended her undefeated record in international figure skating competition, while American Isabeau Levito climbed onto the podium at Grand Prix Finland.

Shimada totaled 229.49 points, overcoming a fall on an under-rotated quadruple toe loop in Saturday's free skate to prevail by a comfortable 15.43 points over Niina Petrokina of Estonia.

Levito jumped from sixth after Friday's short program to place third overall, making her ninth career Grand Prix podium in 11 career starts — the highest podium percentage for a U.S. woman since Michelle Kwan.

Grand Prix Finland concludes later Saturday with the men's free skate (10:15 a.m. ET), and free dance (12:25 p.m.), live on Peacock. NBC airs highlights on Sunday from 4-6 p.m.

FIGURE SKATING: Full Results | Broadcast Schedule

Shimada gave Japan a fifth win in five Grand Prix women's singles events this season. The last regular season event is next week in Japan, where Japan's Mone Chiba is the favorite. The only time a nation swept one season's Grand Prix titles in one discipline was when Russia did so in women's singles in 2019.

Shimada, 18, is in her first senior season after going undefeated in four junior international seasons, which included a record four world junior titles. She spent extra years at the junior level due to age rule changes that left her four months too young to be eligible for the Milan Cortina Olympics.

Shimada ranks second in the world by best total score this season behind countrywoman and Olympic bronze medalist Ami Nakai, who also won her two Grand Prix regular season starts.

Shimada and Nakai will go head-to-head at December's Grand Prix Final, which takes the top per discipline from the six-event Grand Prix regular season, where the world's top skaters each compete twice.

Petrokina, the two-time European champion, became the first Estonian to qualify for a Grand Prix Final with her two runner-up finishes this season.

Levito has a slim chance to qualify for her third Grand Prix Final, but must wait to see how the last regular season event in Japan plays out next week. She needs Chiba to finish outside the top five and Yuna Aoki to finish outside the top two.

Earlier in pairs, Germans Annika Hocke and Robert Kunkel overtook short program leaders Lia Pereira and Trennt Michaud of Canada by 1.12 points for their second Grand Prix title after 2023 Skate America.

Americans Audrey Shin and Spencer Howe and Katie McBeath and Balazs Nagy were third and fourth.

Shin and Howe posted the best overall score for a U.S. pair this season, supplanting the total from two-time U.S. champions Alisa Efimova and Misha Mitrofanov from Skate Canada.

McBeath and Nagy, who were third at Saatva Skate America, were third in the short in Finland, then dropped behind Shin and Nagy after the free skate. That hurt their chances of qualifying for the Final, which will be decided by other skaters' finishes at next week's NHK Trophy.

#2836. Giants are Colts' next opponent, and John Harbaugh is taken aback by Daniel Jones news

Giants coach John Harbaugh is preparing right now for Sunday’s game against the Jaguars. But a week later, he’ll be coaching against the Colts. And he was taken by surprise today when he found out that the Colts have cut quarterback Daniel Jones.

Harbaugh was visibly surprised at his press conference today when a reporter informed him that Jones has been released, and he said when asked about the work his advance scouts are doing on the Colts that it will certainly change things.

“Yeah, probably pretty dramatically,” Harbaugh said. “I mean, wow. I don’t want to get ahead of ourselves here. You just hit me with that.”

Harbaugh said the Giants will still watch plenty of tape of the Colts’ offense this season, but they’ll also have to watch tape of Anthony Richardson.

“The offense will be the offense they run, but it will be centered around the quarterback, for sure,” Harbaugh said. “And they’re probably different styles to some degree, so we’ll have to look at that when the time comes. We have people doing that this week. They’ll have to make a left turn, obviously. Thanks for the info.”

Richardson is a great athlete with a big arm, and the Colts loved him coming out of Florida, and he’ll now have until the end of the season to show whether he can in any way resemble the quarterback the Colts thought they were getting when they took him fourth overall in the 2023 NFL draft. Harbaugh’s Giants will be the first defense Richardson faces for his first meaningful playing time in two years.

#2835. Utah Jazz vs Phoenix Suns: Player Grades

By my money, this was the most disappointing loss of the year thus far. Riding high after two road wins, the team looked exhilarated for the first time all year and the home fans were excited to finally have something to latch onto. You could feel how badly the crowd wanted to root hard for their team, but instead, their team simply decided to not show up. The Jazz only lost by 10 points tonight, but if you could only analyze the game based off of body language and effort, you would guess that it was more than that. They didn't hustle after loose balls, they made no attempts to patch their poor decision making, and they looked defeated the whole night. This was a bad loss, and the grades will represent that – not a lot of positives you can take from this one.

Note: These grades are more-or-less relative to the player. An A grade for Darryn Peterson is different than an A grade for Tony Bradley.

It is a make or miss league, and Ace Bailey missed. Honestly, that isn't too surprising; it's been the status quo up to this point in the season. One of the most underrated skills in the league, fascinatingly enough, is actually making shots – a reputation alone doesn't help quite as much. To this point, Ace has a limit on his ceiling until his jump shots go in at a consistent rate, which has not been the case in his collegiate or NBA career. He will rarely go 0-7, but a better shooter will have nights like this even less frequently. Additionally, his defense has become less and less impressive as the year has progressed; tonight, he wasn't nearly as loud as he was at the beginning of the year. Without event creation on defense, self creation on offense, or the ability to hit open threes, Ace really isn't providing anything. Will Hardy recognized this, benching Ace for the closing stretch. This team's potential is very tightly knit to Ace's, and games like this just won't cut it.

After heaps of well-deserved praise after his recent stretch, Mo Bamba had a major regression-t0-the-mean game tonight. He was essentially invisible; the Suns were attacking him relentlessly and he was unable to use his size to make them pay. He looked comfortable taking threes…perhaps too comfortable; I think I prefer when he takes them with defenders sagging off completely, instead of in the face of solid closeouts. Ultimately, this harsh grade was given in the light of his recent games. These were the minutes I expected we'd get from Mo when the Jazz picked him up initially, but because he's shown me more, I've come to expect more. So, while I've loved the Bamba experience thus far, this game, hopefully, acted as a return to reality for those already championing Bamba as the Jazz's answer at the center position.

This was one of the worst offensive games that I can remember from Keyonte. The missed shots were one thing, but the lack of effort and body language were really discouraging. Keyonte has taken on the mantle of leader, but the responsibilities of that title are not relinquished during games where his threes are not going in. It's fun to be a leader when you beat the Lakers in L.A., but it's essential to be a leader when you are battling the Suns in a slog two nights later, and you are simply not doing that when you nonchalantly let opponents steal offensive rebounds from under your nose. I restrained myself from delivering an F due to some genuinely solid defensive contests, but I am placing the majority of this game's ugliness squarely on Keyonte's shoulders.

Josh Green is Josh Green. He can't dribble or pass, which is detrimental in lineups where he is playing alongside 2-3 other players who also can't dribble or pass. But he'll hit an open corner three or two, and he is easily our best defensive option against any star scorer, ranging from 6'4 to 6'8. Devin Booker ended with 23 points, but he wasn't particularly efficient, partially because Green understood that the reason he gets minutes is to make life difficult for the bucket-getter that he's lined up against. The final ATO was his worst coverage of the night, but that did not define his night – we did not lose this game because of Josh Green losing Devin Booker at the end of the game.

Very impressive night from Jaxson Hayes, where he reminded the fanbase that, until this season, he was a far more established player than Mo Bamba for a reason. One underrated skill of his is his timing on his rolls to the rim – he plays it at just the right pace where the ball-handler is able to hit him right in the most sensitive pocket of the defense, and tonight the Suns didn't really have an answer besides fouling. The missed free throws were frustrating, and I don't feel like Hayes ever plays as a 7 footer on defense (more of a shot blocker than a rim protector, and tonight he wasn't even blocking shots), but providing a consistent and efficient offensive outlet is something that shouldn't be undersold.

People aren't too quick to offer Svi praise these days, but he provided one of the better combinations of shooting and effort on the team tonight. I get it, I get it, his turnovers are frustrating, especially for a player that really shouldn't be dribbling, but tonight he understood that someone had to make something happen, which helps me give him a bit of a pass for his 3 turnovers tonight. At the end of the day, if Svi can make jump shots, he's a solid role player – let's hope that his recent efficiency continues on throughout the season.

Maybe I should've spent more attention on what Okogie was doing on defense, but it didn't pop off the screen tonight like it sometimes does. Okogie is somehow an even more simplistic player than Green – if he doesn't have a small, not-too-quick guard to pester, and the shot's not dropping, he really isn't providing too much to the team. He doesn't take much away – he'll shoot when open, and bring intensity at the very least – but he didn't contribute much of anything to the team tonight.

Interesting game for Peterson. He went 0-7 from three, which could make you think that I'd penalize him the same way I did Ace and Keyonte, but Peterson was able to get his fingerprints all over the game tonight in a way that his other prestigious youngster teammates could not. Some of his passes tonight were legitimately inspiring, and gave me hope that his long-term position might actually be point guard – his one hand dart to Green in the corner after a shifty drive was particularly impressive. Additionally, even though he struggled when matched up with a shifty point guard in Gillespie, I still think he gave some serious defensive flashes, at the very least on the defensive glass. Ultimately, I think much of a player's value is based on what they can provide to a team when their shot isn't falling, and tonight, Peterson provided a bit of everything while Ace and Keyonte did not. Some nights will be off nights, but that can be overcome with skills that are not dependent on the variance inherent to jump shots – Peterson seems to already have a strong understanding of that fact.

The defense wasn't awesome, and the decision making wasn't always great, but when he is making his shots, Brice provides so much value to this team because we simply do not have a surplus of players who can do what he does. He is a versatile shot-maker who can succeed even when faced with contests, and that cannot be said for a lot of the players competing for his minutes. Until we get some other player who can fill this niche on the Jazz bench, I can't get behind the prevailing sentiment, which is to call for his immediate dismissal from the consistently active Jazzmen. Brice brings an NBA skill that can completely alter a team's fortunes, which he did tonight – without his efficient shooting, I think the Jazz would've been dead in the water.

Who stood out for you? Which grades would you change? Sound off below!

#2834. Future Broncos: 3 Prospects to Watch in Week 12 of NCAA Football Season

Welcome back to another installment of Future Broncos. We are getting down to the final weeks of the collegiate football season before the playoffs and bowl games begin. Another Saturday, another big slate of games for fans to enjoy. Here are three prospects of interest Broncos Country should keep their eye on today.

The Broncos tried to make a splash prior to the NFL trade deadline making a deal for Los Angeles Chargers linebacker Daiyan Henley. Unfortunately, the medical review of Henley's knee wasn't favorable and the trade wound up being nixed. That saved the Broncos a fourth and fifth round selection in the 2027 NFL Draft. However, it's clear they are hoping to upgrade the position long-term given their interest in Henley.

I haven't covered too many inside linebackers in this year's class. However, UCLA prospect Sammy Omosigho is one that is skyrocketing up draft boards. Omosigho started his collegiate career at Oklahoma back in 2023 and spent his first three seasons there.

He never earned a full-time starting role in their defense but was a quality contributor in the snaps he played. In 36 games with the Sooners, he amassed 96 tackles, 10 tackles for loss, 2 sacks, 1 forced fumble, and one fumble recovery. For his final season, he transferred to UCLA and has become one of the best playmakers in their defense.

A ten-game starter for the Bruins, Omosigho (6'2", 239 pounds) has been on an absolute tear as a senior. He already has 72 tackles, 10.5 tackles for loss, 5.5 sacks, and a whopping five forced fumbles. Whether it's stuffing the run, getting after the passer, or doing well in coverage—he touts some of the best numbers of any draft eligible prospect.

Given his versatility, I think he is a player Defensive Coordinator Vance Joseph and the Broncos' scouts are going to be keeping a close eye on throughout the draft evaluation process. You can catch Omosigho in action today when the undefeated and fourth ranked Bruins take on the Michigan Wolverines on CBS. If he keeps playing at a high level, he should easily be a Day 2 selection next April.

This past week, the Broncos opted to extend boundary cornerback Riley Moss. The $67 million deal will keep him in orange and blue for the next for seasons. While Denver's boundary duo is set for the foreseeable future, the long-term outlook at safety is far less certain.

The reason is simple; Brandon Jones is a free agent and has regressed this season. Moreover, Talanoa Hufanga's contract is up after the 2027 season. Keep in mind most all of their depth players are on one-year contracts as well. There is no doubt in my mind Denver will be addressing the long-term need there in the 2027 NFL Draft.

One player that is having a tremendous senior season is LSU safety Ty Benefield. Benefield is the son of Daved Benefield, who was a thirteen-year linebacker and All-Star player in the Canadian Football League. Ty was a backup player and pinch starter as a freshman for the Broncos in 2023 but earned a full-time role as a starter for his sophomore and junior campaigns.

In three seasons at Boise State, he played in 41 games with 33 starts and put up some eye-popping numbers: 235 tackles, 18 tackles for loss, 3 sacks, 5 interceptions, 4 fumble recoveries, and 2 forced fumbles. This year, he transferred to LSU to see if he could improve his draft stock and battle much tougher competition in the SEC.

So far, so good for Benefield (6'3", 216 pounds). In my opinion, has been one of the best defensive backs in the nation. In ten games, he has 49 tackles, 4.5 sacks and 3 interceptions. With some injuries in their secondary, he has seen time at both safety and nickelback. That positional versatility will be valued by NFL scouts and general managers and should be of interest to Denver giving the ambiguity at both positions moving forward.

His physical profile is off the charts, and he has consistently produced at a high level. I think he is one of the most well-rounded defensive backs in this class and a great fit for Joseph's aggressive defense. If he keeps dominating, I don't think being picked in the Top 50 is out of the question for him come spring. You can catch Benefield in action this afternoon when the Tigers take on the ninth-ranked Tennessee Volunteers on ABC.

I haven't covered too many running backs in Future Broncos. I remain quite bullish on the potential of Jonah Coleman and RJ Harvey as the future thunder and lightning duo for the offense. However, both have had some injuries this season and J.K. Dobbins has not looked as good as he did in the first half of 2025 prior to injury.

I kind of treat running back in drafts like MLB teams approach pitching. It's always worth pursuing and better to have and need than need and not have. Strong rushing attacks are what get you through the late half of the season and are extremely important come the playoffs.

A particular underclassman that has caught my eyes in a few games this season is Utah Utes junior Wayshawn Parker. Parker started his career out at Washington State. In twelve contests as a freshman, he had 137 carries for 735 yards and 4 touchdowns. He added 11 receptions for 108 yards and a touchdown as a receiver.

For his sophomore season, he transferred to Utah and saw an uptick in playing production. Parker had 981 yards and 6 touchdowns on 149 carries. His 6.6 yard per carry average was the best in the Big 12. He caught 13 passes for 185 yards and 3 touchdowns and had 1,166 all-purpose yards.

In 10 games this season, Parker (6'0″, 199 pounds) has 764 rushing yards and 9 rushing touchdowns. He also has been more involved in their passing attack under the new coaching regime and sports 29 catches for 269 yards and 4 touchdowns.

Based on what I've seen, he is one of the best draft-eligible backs when it comes to forcing missed tackles (49 last season) and getting yards after contact. He is a dual threat player that offers a lot of value as a runner and receiver out of the backfield. For those of you who are wondering about his pass protection? It's quite good for just a junior player.

I'm not sure Parker ends up declaring, but he is definitely on my radar. I think he would add a lot of juice and playmaking ability for the Broncos. He has been a big reason for Utah's success on offense this season. You can catch Parker and the second-ranked Utes in action when they take on TCU tomorrow evening.

Jets quarterback Geno Smith (ankle) was a full participant at Friday’s practice and has no injury designation.

#2832. Will Steelers WR Michael Pittman Jr. play in Week 11 vs. Eagles?

Michael Pittman Jr. is a 5,000-plus-yard wide receiver who held a special place in the hearts of Indianapolis Colts fans for six years. Traded to the Steelers in March 2026, Pittman has had his early tenure with Pittsburgh hampered by nagging health setbacks — but he has a legitimate possibility of suiting up this Sunday against the Philadelphia Eagles.

Using his 2026 injury history and the Week 11 practice report to help identify whether or not Pittman can return to action this week, here's everything fans need to know about the veteran wide receiver's potential availability.

Pittman has dealt with a lengthy 2026 injury history, as the Steelers receiver suffered a foot setback which forced him to miss significant time. He returned in Week 3 but reaggravated the injury during Week 5 practice and was subsequently placed on injured reserve. In total, Pittman has missed six games due to this foot injury.

Steelers fans may have a lot to celebrate this Sunday, as Pittman was able to begin practice on Wednesday as a limited participant before upgrading to full participation on Thursday and Friday. While he is still listed as questionable to play in Week 11, finishing practice as a full participant on the final two days of practice points towards him making his return in Week 11 against the Eagles.

#2831. Nets’ Moritz Wagner disappointed after losing to brother in ‘one of the more conflicting games’

Brooklyn's Moritz Wagner had been in the NBA for nine years, alive for 20 more, and never played against his brother Franz in a game that counted.

This first one stung, having to watch his younger brother torch his team for 35 points, including a game-winner with 2.8 seconds left in a game the Nets had to have.

"I don't care about him. I wanted to win the game. It's one of the more conflicting games I've ever been a part of," Moritz, who scored three points in 22 minutes, said afterward. "That is weird, but we've got enough problems going on. I can't worry about nothing on the other side. I'm trying to win the game, and that one hurts."

Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner (13) defends against Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner (22) in Brooklyn, N.Y., Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

The Nets had already dropped three straight, including Wednesday's humiliation against Chicago that prompted a team meeting.

Brooklyn jumped to a quick 17-11 lead against Orlando, then coughed up a 27-8 run.

The younger Wagner tore up Brooklyn's defense, including 14 points in the third quarter alone. But it was his 15-footer that won it with 2.8 seconds remaining.

"He's a great player. He's gonna be an All-Star many times in this career. And great players make great plays," Moritz said. "I don't think we lost the game on that play. There are plenty of opportunities. We turned it over twice down the stretch. We took some questionable shots, didn't make enough plays, and that's what happens. Great player, make great plays."

Moritz played for Michigan and was a 2018 first-round pick by Orlando, where he'd spent his entire career until inking with the Nets this summer.

Center Moritz Wagner reacts during the second half of the Nets' 113-111 loss to the Magic on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

Franz, four years his junior, had followed in his footsteps and starred for the Wolverines before being picked eighth overall by the Magic in 2021. He even lived with Moe until the latter came to Brooklyn in the summer.

"To be honest, it was one of the toughest things I've ever had to feel on a court. Too much going on, I was very anxious first half, clearly (not) focused on the game. I didn't expect it would be this difficult," said Moritz. "I'm glad we got this one out the way. I'm not sure how I'm gonna deal with that; to be determined. But we did it, and now it's not a big deal anymore."

Franz said: "My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him. But as weird as it (was) … it's a blessing to share the court with your family. Not a lot of people get to do that so that's how I look at it."

Orlando forward Franz Wagner (22) celebrates during the Magic's win over the Nets on Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center. Robert Sabo for NY Post

Starting point guard Nolan Traore left with a right hamstring injury in the third quarter and didn't return.

Mikel Brown Jr. had a career-high-tying 18 points, including a dozen in that third period.

Danny Wolf started and had 20 points, the third-most in his career. Egor Dëmin came off the bench for the first time since November of his rookie season.

Paolo Banchero (right ankle) was out for Orlando.

#2830. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.
